In the style of Rolls-Royce, a monumental upright limousine with a tall flat-topped radiator shell of polished steel slats crowned by a small winged lady mascot, long bonnet, flat slab sides, and a formal near-vertical windscreen. Rear-hinged coach doors, thin chrome swage line along the flanks, large round or rectangular headlamps set wide beside the grille, and tall narrow wheels with plain polished hubcaps carrying a small double-R badge. Two-tone paint is classic, deep claret, silver sand, midnight blue, or black over silver. Interior is hand-stitched cream or tan leather, deep lambswool carpets, burr walnut veneer with fine inlay, chrome organ-stop vents, slim thin-rimmed steering wheel and a pleated headlining. Mood is hushed, imposing, dignified, with mirror-polished brightwork and long smooth rolling proportions.
In the style of Bentley, a muscular British grand tourer with a broad mesh grille in a chrome surround topped by a winged B badge, powerful haunches, long bonnet and a short rear deck. Round paired headlamps flank the grille, a deep sculpted shoulder line runs the full body, and the stance is low, wide and heavy on large alloy wheels. Early open tourers wear dark British racing green paint with leather bonnet straps, wire-spoke wheels, cutaway doors and a flat riveted scuttle. Modern coupes show hand-polished chrome, diamond-quilted leather seats in contrasting hide colours, knurled metal switchgear, organ-stop vent pulls, polished burr walnut or open-pore wood veneer, and a rotating dashboard panel. Colours are deep green, dark blue, silver, black and burgundy.
In the style of Jaguar in the 1960s, a long low sensuous sports car with a very long sweeping bonnet, tight cabin set far back, oval open-mouth grille, and fared-in headlamps under smooth glass or sloping wings. Flowing curved fenders, tapering fastback tail, delicate chrome bumpers, slim pillars, and wire-spoke wheels with knock-off spinners. A leaping cat mascot or growling cat face badge sits on the nose. Paint in British racing green, signal red, cream, metallic silver blue and opalescent grey. Interior has bucket seats in red or tan leather, polished wooden dashboard, a row of toggle switches and large chrome-ringed white-faced dials, and a slim wood-rimmed steering wheel. Mood is elegant speed, curvaceous and feline.
In the style of Aston Martin, a lean British grand tourer with a long bonnet, short rear overhang, and a distinctive wide trapezoid grille with horizontal chrome or black slats, framed in a bright surround. Side strakes or gill vents behind the front wheels, a low rakish roofline falling into a fastback or kamm tail, and a muscular flowing rear haunch. Early cars use round headlamps and wire wheels, modern ones slim swept LED lamps and a crisp rising shoulder line. A winged badge sits at nose and tail. Paint in silver birch, racing green, midnight blue, Caledonian blue and gunmetal. Interior is hand-stitched leather with quilted panels, contrast piping, bridge-of-weld metal trim, polished glass switches and fine wood or carbon fibre veneer. Mood is sleek, refined, quietly aggressive.
In the style of Mini in the 1960s, a tiny boxy two-box hatchback saloon with a wheel pushed to each corner, short overhangs, upright flat nose and tail, and a roof like a small lid. Round chrome headlamps on the front panel, a thin oval or chrome-frame grille, external welded seams along the body edges, exposed door hinges, sliding windows, and slim chrome bumpers. Tiny ten-inch wheels with steel discs and bright chrome trim rings. Paint in tartan red, island blue, Old English white, British racing green, with a contrasting white roof and stripes over the bonnet. Interior is minimal, with a central round speedometer pod, rubber floor mats, thin-spoked wheel, ribbed vinyl seats and a toggle switch row. Mood is cheeky, compact and playful.
In the style of Land Rover, a rugged slab-sided utility four-wheel-drive vehicle with flat aluminium panels, an upright bluff nose, and a plain rectangular grille between round headlamps set into the wings. Square bonnet with exposed hinges, boxy cab, squared wheel arches, a spare wheel on the bonnet or tailgate, and a short stubby stance on chunky treaded tyres on steel or plain alloy wheels. Roofs have a safari-style roof rack or canvas top, with a contrast white roof on later estates. Paint in matt olive green, bronze green, sand, poppy red and pale grey. Interior is spartan, with painted metal dash, bare rubber matting, simple dials and tough fabric seats. Mood is honest, utilitarian and farmyard-tough.
In the style of Lotus, an extremely low, light and tiny British sports car with a fibreglass shell, a sharp wedge or rounded teardrop nose, pop-up or small round headlamps, and a high tapering tail. Roofline barely above the knee, wide haunches over small wheels, bare minimal bumpers, and a round green and yellow badge. Paint in British racing green with a bright yellow stripe, bright yellow, white, orange and metallic silver. Alloy wheels are small and finely spoked or in cast star form. Interior is basic and cramped, with thin bucket seats in cloth or vinyl, exposed aluminium, small instruments under the dash, and a leather-wrapped wheel. Mood is stripped, minimal, purposeful and track-bred.
In the style of Morgan, a hand-built British roadster in a deliberately pre-war manner, with a long louvred bonnet, separate sweeping cycle-type wings, a flat narrow cockpit and a rounded tail. Tall upright chrome radiator grille with a slim vertical bar, freestanding round chrome headlamps on stalks, a spare wheel on the rear, and wire-spoke wheels with knock-off hubs. Body panels are aluminium over an ash wood frame, with a folding canvas hood and leather straps over the bonnet. Paint in racing green, claret, cream, navy and ivory, with polished brass and chrome details. Interior has simple leather bucket seats, a polished wooden dash with round white-faced dials and pull-out switches, and a thin-rimmed wooden wheel. Mood is charming, handcrafted and nostalgic.
In the style of Austin in the 1930s to 1950s, a rounded, upright and sensible British family saloon with a tall chrome radiator grille, curved separate wings, running boards on early cars, and large round headlamps on the wings. Bonnet with side louvres or a long hinged lid, rounded boot, narrow bumpers with overriders, and a bulbous trunk. Thin steel wheels with chrome hubcaps and a winged badge on the grille. Paint in black, maroon, deep green, grey, cream and two-tone shades. Interior has beige or maroon leather, polished wood dashboard with a small central speedometer, spindly steering wheel with a large rim, and thick carpet. Mood is stately, modest and homely.
In the style of Triumph sports cars in the 1960s, a compact open two-seat roadster with a long low bonnet, a wedge-shaped rear and a tidy tapering tail with chrome bumpers. Rounded sculpted wings with a pronounced crisp crease, headlamps tucked into a smooth nose, a small egg-crate or mesh grille with a round shield badge, and a folding black cloth hood. Wheels are wire-spoke or steel with chrome trim rings. Paint in signal red, white, British racing green, pale yellow, and deep blue. Interior has black or red vinyl bucket seats, a wood or painted dashboard with round dials and toggle switches, a wood-rim wheel and a floor gearshift. Mood is breezy, sporty and affordable.
In the style of MG, a small British open sports car with a tall upright radiator grille of chrome vertical slats under an octagon badge, a long flat louvred bonnet, cutaway doors, cycle wings and a rounded tail with a spare wheel. Round headlamps stand either side of the radiator, and a low folding windscreen sits above the cockpit. Later 1960s cars have a smooth body, chrome bumpers and wire-spoke wheels. Paint in racing green, red, cream, ivory and pale blue. Interior has bucket seats in red or black leather, an engine-turned or wooden dashboard with a tachometer and speedometer in chrome rims, and a slim banjo-spoke wheel. Mood is jaunty, spirited and cheerful.
In the style of Volkswagen in the 1950s and 1960s, a rounded beetle-shaped car with a domed roof, curved smooth bonnet, round bug-eye headlamps standing up on the wings, and sweeping separate fenders ending in running boards. The body is one smooth continuous curve with a tiny oval rear window, no grille at the front, and slim chrome bumpers. Small steel wheels with chrome hubcaps and a round badge on the nose. Paint in pastel beige, sea blue, coral red, mint green, ivory and cream, plus grey. Interior has a bare painted metal dash with a single round dial, thin two-spoke wheel, cloth or vinyl seats, and exposed painted door panels. Mood is cute, honest, friendly and economical.
In the style of Porsche, a compact rear-engined sports coupe with a sloping fastback roofline that falls from the windscreen to the tail, round headlamps rising from flared front wings, a low rounded nose, and wide muscular rear haunches. A smooth teardrop profile, a tail with a thin full-width light bar in modern versions, short overhangs, and a small retractable or fixed rear spoiler. Wheels are five-spoke alloys or forged star designs. Paint in guards red, silver grey, signal orange, racing yellow, gulf blue and black. A crest badge with a rearing horse sits on the nose. Interior has five round dials in a row under a deep hood, black leather and houndstooth cloth, aluminium trim, and a small leather wheel. Mood is purposeful, balanced and athletic.
In the style of BMW in the 1970s and 1980s, a compact sporty saloon or coupe with a crisp three-box shape, thin pillars, large glasshouse, and a low nose with a twin-kidney grille of two slim vertical oval openings with chrome bars. Four round headlamps in pairs, a sharp shoulder line running nose to tail, slim chrome or black bumpers, and a short boot. Slight forward-leaning shark nose stance. Cross-spoke alloy or steel wheels with a blue and white roundel hub badge. Paint in polaris silver, bahama blue, henna red, black, white and fjord grey. Interior has a driver-angled dashboard, black cloth or leather, round clear dials, plain plastic and brushed trim. Mood is precise, driver-focused and understated.
In the style of Mercedes-Benz in the 1960s to 1980s, an upright, dignified and solid saloon with a tall chrome radiator grille crowned by a three-pointed star mascot on the bonnet, wide chrome surround, vertical-oriented tail lamps, and thin pillars with a large glasshouse. Vertical rectangular or large round headlamps, a flat-sided body with sharp creases, chunky chrome bumpers with rubber strips, and ribbed tail lamps. Wheels are steel with chrome caps or small alloy wheels with a distinctive pattern. Paint in champagne, ivory, signal red, midnight blue, silver grey and black. Interior has wide tufted velour or leather seats, walnut wood trim, large rotary controls, and a big four-spoke steering wheel with a padded centre. Mood is solid, formal and engineered.
In the style of Audi, a clean, precise and technical saloon with a large single-frame hexagonal grille with horizontal bars, framed in a chrome or black surround, and four interlocking rings badge on the nose. Sharp tight surfaces, taut shoulder line, flush window glass, and sharp LED headlamps with a distinct daytime-light signature. Slim roofline, short overhangs, and a tailgate with a wide light bar. Multi-spoke or five-arm alloy wheels. Paint in glacier white, daytona grey, mythos black, tango red, ibis white and navy blue. Interior has a flat machined look with aluminium trim, perfect panel gaps, round air vents, black or grey leather, a flat-bottom wheel, and a virtual display. Mood is crisp, taut and engineered.
In the style of Opel in the 1960s and 1970s, a neat German coupe or saloon with a wide low nose, a rectangular black-mesh grille with a lightning bolt badge, and a coke-bottle waist with a rising line toward the tail. Round or rectangular headlamps, small chrome bumpers, thin pillars, and a gently curved fastback. Pop-out flared wheel arches in sports models, tail lamps in neat rectangular clusters, and steel wheels with chrome hubcaps or small alloy wheels. Paint in signal yellow, bright orange, lime green, white, and sky blue. Interior has bucket seats in checked cloth or vinyl, a simple dashboard with round dials, and a thin wheel. Mood is compact, sporty and mass-market stylish.
In the style of Citroen in the 1950s to 1970s, a futuristic streamlined saloon with a low teardrop body, a sloping shark nose, a smooth aerodynamic tail tapering to a narrow rear, and a car that seems to crouch on its haunches. Rear wheel arches covered by smooth skirts, single-spoke steering wheel, and thin pillars. Double chevron badge on the nose, a minimal grille slot, and headlamps behind a smooth glass cover that turn with the steering. Roof in white fibreglass, body in light pastel colours like pale green, ivory, grey blue, and sand beige, or in bold orange and brown. Interior has a very low bench seat, cloth in checks, a pod-shaped instrument panel, and odd plastic controls. Mood is quirky, avant-garde and comfortable.
In the style of Peugeot in the 1980s and 1990s, a neat, compact, well-proportioned hatchback or saloon with a clean wedge profile, a lion standing on its hind legs badge on the nose, and a rectangular black grille between slim wraparound headlamps. Smooth uncluttered flanks, a single crisp body line along the doors, black plastic bumpers, tidy rectangular tail lamps spanning the tail, and a rising beltline. Wheels are neat alloy designs with five or six holes. Paint in white, bright red, ocean blue, silver metallic, dark green and black. Interior has deep-pile cloth seats, a simple dash with a round binnacle of clear dials, grey plastic and thin stalks. Mood is tidy, practical and sporty.
In the style of Renault in the 1970s and 1980s, a compact boxy hatchback with a square nose, flat bonnet, huge flat rectangular headlamps, and a large diamond-shaped badge in the centre of a flat black grille. Steel wheels with small plastic hubcaps or small alloy wheels. Paint in bright orange, yellow, vivid red, light blue, off-white and beige metallic. Interior has bold striped or check cloth seats, a flat dashboard in black or brown plastic, a round instrument binnacle and big stalks. Mood is cheerful, functional and French-practical.
In the style of Bugatti in the 1930s, a sensuous coachbuilt sports car dominated by a tall horseshoe-shaped radiator grille in polished nickel, with a small red oval badge with white lettering. In the style of Delahaye in the 1930s, a flamboyant French coachbuilt coupe or cabriolet with very long, sweeping lines, a teardrop-shaped body, pontoon wings that flow over the wheels, and a long bonnet running into a sloping tail. Wheels are pierced discs or wire spokes with chrome hub caps. Paint in two-tone combinations such as ivory over dark blue, silver over burgundy, and deep black. Interior has cream or red leather, an engine-turned dash with round dials, and a delicate wheel. Mood is theatrical, curvaceous and Art Deco.

In the style of Maserati, a graceful Italian grand tourer with a long bonnet, tight fastback or coupe roofline, and a large oval grille with vertical chrome bars bearing a trident badge. In the style of Buick, 1950s American cars with a toothy chrome grille like a wide smile, large round chrome bumper guards, and a row of three or four round portholes on each front fender. A sweeping chrome side spear that dips down along the doors, a slightly bulbous body with curved glass and whitewall tyres. Bright wire or chrome-covered wheels with a tri-shield badge. Cabins of plush two-tone fabric with a wide chrome dash and a big round speedometer. Colours of cream, sea green, coral and maroon. Comfortable middle-class luxury, rounded and heavily chromed, dignified rather than flashy.
In the style of Pontiac, 1960s and 70s American muscle cars with a split twin-nostril grille divided by a body-coloured beak, stacked or hidden headlamps, a long hood with a raised scoop, and a coke-bottle waistline that swells over the rear wheels. Tall rear haunches, a spoiler on the trunk, bold stripes, and a red arrowhead badge. Rally wheels in brushed metal with bright trim rings, black vinyl bucket seats and a deep engine-turned dash with round gauges. Colours of Carousel red, Verdoro green, Cameo ivory and Orbit orange, plus a screaming bird decal on a black hood. Aggressive, swaggering street-racer attitude.
In the style of Dodge, early 1970s American muscle with a wide aggressive stance, a long flat hood with twin bulging scoops, and a split grille with a hidden-lamp or twin round headlamp face. Squared-off fenders with a pronounced coke-bottle hip, a bold bumblebee stripe or tail band, and a ducktail rear spoiler. Steel wheels with a slotted rim and a fat white-letter tyre, a pistol-grip shifter inside, black vinyl bucket seats and a strong gauge cluster. Colours of high-impact violet, lime green, Plum Crazy purple, bright orange and sublime green. Brutal, loud, cartoonish tough-guy look with ram or red stripe badge.
In the style of Chrysler, late 1950s and early 60s American cars of the forward look, very low and wide with tall swept-back tail fins, a long slender profile and a huge expanse of glass in a wraparound windshield. A wide mouth grille with a bright bar, canted dual headlamps in chrome pods, and flowing side chrome with a contrasting colour panel. A pentastar or winged badge, big round tail lamps, and wheel covers with a radial spoke look. Interiors with push-button gearshift on the dash, a bold sculpted panel and luxurious fabric. Colours of turquoise, flamingo pink, cream and charcoal. Space-age, optimistic and airy.
In the style of Lincoln, 1960s American flagship luxury sedans with a slab-sided, perfectly flat body, a thin knife-edge beltline and a restrained bright grille of fine horizontal bars. Rear-hinged suicide doors, a long level hood, a square formal roofline, four-door convertible tops, and a star badge in a plain rectangle. Minimal chrome, just a thin strip along the shoulder and ringed bumpers. Slim vertical tail lamps and plain wheel covers. Inside, a flat dashboard with brushed metal, deep leather and a feeling of grave dignity. Colours of Presidential black, midnight blue, silver and white. Understated, sober, expensive.
In the style of Jeep, rugged utilitarian off-road vehicles with a flat boxy body, a seven-slot upright grille flanked by round headlamps, exposed hinges and bolts, flat folding windshield, squared-off wheel arches and short overhangs. Removable doors and soft canvas or hard top, a spare tyre mounted on the tailgate, and heavy black plastic fender flares. Painted steel bumpers, tall knobby tyres on steel or black alloy wheels, and tow hooks in red. Interior with hose-down panels, grab handles and bare painted metal. Colours of olive drab, sand, desert tan, safety yellow and bright orange. Honest, military-derived, adventure look.
In the style of Tesla, minimalist electric cars with smooth unbroken body surfaces, a closed-off nose with no grille, a low sloping fastback roofline and flush door handles. Slim LED headlamps and a thin horizontal tail light bar, a large glass roof, frameless windows, and a plain T-shaped badge. Aerodynamic turbine-style wheels with a flat cover, body-coloured trim and almost no chrome. The interior is a stark white or black cabin with a single huge landscape touchscreen, a wooden or matte strip, no instrument cluster and no buttons. Colours of pearl white, solid black, deep blue metallic, midnight silver and red multicoat. Clean, futuristic, software-first.
In the style of Studebaker, early 1950s American coupes with a pointed bullet nose like an aircraft spinner at the centre of the grille, a low wide stance and a thin wraparound rear window. Slim body with a long low hood, a low roofline, an almost European flat-sided silhouette, and a gently tapering tail. Chrome bumpers, small fins, and round tail lamps. Late 1950s versions with gold or turquoise hardtops and a long low wagon shape. Pale pastel two-tone paint in pink and white, sky blue and cream. Inside, a streamlined dash with a big round dial and brushed metal. Quirky, futuristic, a bit strange.
In the style of Packard, 1930s and 40s American luxury cars with a tall narrow ox-yoke grille shaped like a pointed shield with fine vertical bars, a very long hood, sweeping separate fenders and chrome-ringed headlamps on the fender tops. Chrome flying mascot or cormorant on the radiator, wire or disc wheels with whitewall tyres, running boards, a long wheelbase and a formal upright cabin. Deep gloss paint in black, maroon, dark green and cream with a thin coach line. Interiors of walnut veneer, mohair or leather and elegant engine-turned metal. Stately, hushed, old-money elegance.
In the style of Hudson, late 1940s and early 1950s American cars with a step-down body, a very low floor and a bulbous slab-sided shape with rounded fenders. A wide, low, simple chrome grille with a triangle badge, a smooth slab trunk, a fastback roofline and skirted rear wheels. Small windows, a heavy brow over the headlamps and a rounded tail with narrow lamps. Two-tone paint in maroon and cream, deep green and tan. Chrome hubcaps with whitewalls and a thin chrome strip along the flank. Inside, a deep seat, a big steering wheel, a plain dash. Hunkered, bathtub-shaped, solid look.
In the style of Oldsmobile, 1950s and early 60s American cars with a rocket motif, a bright wide grille with a central round intake like a jet engine, a chrome rocket-ship ornament on the hood and a sweeping chrome spear down each flank. Wraparound windshield, a hardtop roof with a thin pillar, small tail fins and bullet-shaped tail lamps. Chrome wheel covers with spinner centres. Interiors of two-tone vinyl and gleaming dash trim with a ribbon speedometer. Colours of rocket red, white, mint green and sky blue. Jet-age, confident, glossy.
In the style of DeSoto, mid to late 1950s American cars with a tall vertical oval grille shaped like a pursed mouth, a huge chrome bumper that wraps around the nose, heavy chrome brows over twin headlamps, and tall pointed tail fins with arrow-shaped lamp trim. Long sweeping side chrome spear splits the two-tone paint, with a hooded roof and a wraparound screen. A wide whitewall tyre and full-disc hubcaps. Interior with a bold dash, push-button shifter and plush fabric. Colours of pink and charcoal, turquoise and white, sunset orange. Dramatic and theatrical.
In the style of Edsel, 1958 American cars with a tall vertical horse-collar grille set in the middle of a wide bumper, a pointed hood crease and a split nose, wingtip boomerang tail lamps and gull-wing chevrons at the rear. Side sculpting with a bright spear, a cut-away dip along the door, and pillarless hardtop coupes with a smooth cabin. Wheels with chrome rim and whitewalls. Interior with rolling-dome speedometer and push buttons on the steering hub. Colours of two-tone pink and black, turquoise and white, yellow and brown. Overwrought, odd, unforgettable.
In the style of Tucker, a late 1940s rear-engined American sedan with a very low, smooth, rounded fastback body like a teardrop, three headlamps with the centre lamp in the nose that turns with the steering wheel, a narrow chrome-bar grille with a wide mouth, and a swept-back roof. Fully enclosed rear with a louvered engine cover, broad rear fenders and slim tail lamps. Chrome bumpers and a thin trim line, with small whitewall tyres and plain hubcaps. Interior with padded dash, central driving position gauge cluster and bench seats. Colours of waltz blue, beige, maroon and dark green. Futuristic and streamlined.
In the style of DeLorean, a 1980s wedge sports coupe with a flat, angular body of bare brushed stainless steel panels with visible grain, no paint, a low long nose and razor-sharp creases. Gull-wing doors that open upward, a flat louvred rear window cover, black plastic bumpers and a rear engine grille. The interior is grey leather with a simple flat dash and rectangular gauge pods. Matte, metallic, sci-fi, raw and unfinished.

In the style of Elie Saab, red-carpet couture gowns that are romantic and feminine, long sheer columns and full-skirted dresses with fitted bodices, plunging necklines, illusion lace, delicate floral applique and beading in gold, silver and crystal. Fabrics are tulle, chiffon, Chantilly lace, silk crepe and satin, in soft powder pink, blush, ivory, champagne, lilac, sky blue, emerald and rich red. Dreamy, luxurious, fairytale mood, shown in soft glowing light on elegant staircases.
In the style of Bonwire kente weaving houses of Ghana, hand-loomed narrow strips of silk and cotton, about four inches wide, woven on a horizontal strip loom and sewn edge to edge into large cloths worn draped over the shoulder. Brilliant geometric patterns of interlocking bands, squares, zigzags, stepped diamonds and blocks in gold, kente yellow, deep red, emerald green, royal blue, black and white. Weft-faced areas alternate with warp-striped plain sections, and supplementary weft motifs create tiny symbols such as ladders and checks. Surfaces are rich, thick, slightly ridged and rhythmic, each strip a different arrangement. Regal, ceremonial, celebratory mood, sunlit courtyard setting with looms and coloured threads.
In the style of Studio 189, Accra-made ethical fashion centred on hand-dyed indigo and hand-batiked cloth, adire-style resist patterns, hand-woven fabrics and block-printed African wax prints. Clothing is relaxed and modern, with wide-leg trousers, boxy tops, flowing wrap dresses, jumpsuits, kimono-style jackets and long skirts in cotton and linen. Palette includes deep indigo blue, white, earthy ochre, rust, black and bright accent colours such as orange and green, in tie-dye swirls, hand-drawn stripes, dots, geometric grids and flowing batik lines. Visible hand-dyed unevenness and crisp artisanal texture. Breezy, warm, socially conscious, sunlit West African mood.
In the style of Maxhosa Africa, South African knitwear built on bold Xhosa beadwork patterns turned into jacquard knit, with sharp geometric bands of triangles, diamonds, chevrons, zigzags, stepped blocks and horizontal stripes. Colours are vibrant and contrasting, such as red, orange, mustard, cobalt blue, emerald, turquoise, pink and black against cream or deep neutral grounds, in rich saturated blocks. Items include chunky sweaters, cardigans, blankets, scarves, beanies, long coats, dresses and trousers in mohair, merino wool and soft cotton. Patterns repeat symmetrically across the chest and sleeves like beaded collars. Warm, proud, celebratory, heritage-meets-contemporary mood.

Glamorous, festive, Afro-modern mood, glossy with sparkle and contrast textures.
In the style of Vlisco, wax-print cotton cloth sold across West and Central Africa, with enormous vividly coloured designs printed identically on both sides with resin, leaving fine crackle veins where dye seeped through the wax. Motifs are oversized flowers, fruit, birds, feathers, fish, keys, eyes, circles, hands, clocks, fans, scrolling vines and bold geometric patterns, outlined and shaded like block-print drawings. Palette is saturated and clashing such as cobalt and orange, scarlet and green, yellow and violet, turquoise and pink, black and white, with the crackle texture. The cloth is cut into wrapped skirts, head ties, tailored dresses and matching sets. Exuberant, expressive, festive market-stall mood.

Playful and intellectual, polished, artful and modern Johannesburg mood, shown in clean gallery light.
In the style of Havaianas, Brazilian rubber flip-flops with a thin flat sole and Y-shaped straps, in glossy matte rubber. Colours are juicy, summery solids such as white, yellow, lime green, hot pink, turquoise, red, navy and orange, with a contrasting colour for the strap against the sole and stripes on the flag-like version. Two-tone, flat, lightweight and slightly squishy, shown against beach sand, blue sea and bright sun. Cheerful, casual and tropical mood.

In the style of Jingdezhen Imperial Kilns, thin-walled white porcelain with a glassy, faintly bluish transparent glaze, painted under the glaze in rich cobalt blue. In the style of Longquan Celadon Kilns, thick-glazed stoneware in a soft, jade-like green, from pale plum and sea green to deep leaf green, with a lustrous, unctuous, slightly translucent finish that pools thicker in recesses. Forms are restrained and sculptural, such as lotus-petal bowls, plum vases with swelling shoulders, tripod incense burners and fish-handled vases. Decoration is minimal, with carved or moulded lotus petals, incised combed lines and sometimes a fine crackle network. Rims fade lighter where the glaze thins, and the unglazed foot ring burns to russet brown. The mood is quiet, scholarly and understated, like polished river stone.
In the style of Ando Cloisonne, Japanese cloisonne vases, boxes and plates with smooth, glossy enamel fields held by hair-thin silver or brass wires. Colours are deep and jewel-like, including midnight blue, black, wisteria violet, jade green, ivory and warm gold, with a mirror finish and no visible wire thickness. Subjects are delicate birds, hydrangea, wisteria, irises, chrysanthemums, butterflies and tiny geometric brocade borders, often floating on a plain black or blue ground with generous empty space. Wireless techniques create soft gradients in petals and feathers. Vessels are slender, with narrow necks, flared mouths and silver rims, and are finished with gilt metal collars.
In the style of Kutani Ware, porcelain painted with dense, opaque overglaze enamels in five dark, saturated colours, namely deep green, Prussian blue, yellow, purple and aubergine red. Designs cover nearly the whole surface with bold landscapes, birds, flowers, geometric diaper patterns and fine black outlines. A second manner uses a bright red body painting with gold, carrying tiny figures and ornate panels in minute detail. Green is often laid on thickly as a background with a heavy, jewel-like gloss. Shapes include plates, sake cups, jars and lidded bowls, with a rich, theatrical, almost tapestry-like richness and a thick bright glaze.
In the style of Arita Imari Kilns, white porcelain decorated in a crowded palette of underglaze cobalt blue, iron red and gold. Panels of chrysanthemums, peonies, pine, bamboo, cranes and phoenix are packed into fan, circle and brocade-pattern reserves that fill every part of the plate, bowl or jar. Black outlines, lacy borders, flaring scalloped rims and lobed forms are typical. Red and blue dominate while green and black add accents, and gilding glows on petals and borders. Lidded jars and large bottles are tall and rounded, giving an opulent, ornate and festive effect.
In the style of Nambu Tekki, cast-iron kettles and teapots with a heavy, squat, rounded body, a dark matte black or rust-brown finish and a lacquered iron-oxide surface. The skin is covered in rows of raised small round studs called arare, or in smooth walls with fine texture. Details are a flat lid with a small knob in brass or iron, a swinging bail handle, a short spout, a firm foot and a thick lip. The surface looks like old weathered metal with a slight sheen, and a few forms are decorated with raised pine, plum or wave motifs. Tea-ceremony restraint, sturdiness and quiet weight define them.
In the style of Wajima-nuri Lacquer Houses, bowls, trays, boxes and chopsticks with thick, glassy, flawless coats of black or deep vermilion urushi lacquer polished to a mirror depth. Surfaces are smooth, with a warm glow, and feel substantial. The foundation shows only in sturdy, rounded edges and neat foot rings. Decoration is restrained, with fine gold maki-e flowers, cranes and grasses, or inlaid chin-kin lines carved into the lacquer and filled with gold leaf. Red interiors contrast with black exteriors. The overall mood is formal, luxurious and calm, with crisp, perfectly even shapes.
In the style of Kamakura-bori, carved wooden trays, bowls, boxes and mirrors cut from katsura or ginkgo and finished in red-brown or black lacquer. Carving is bold and shallow, using gouge cuts to form camellia flowers, peonies, cherry blossoms, leaves and waves, with rounded petal edges and flat bevelled leaf surfaces. The lacquer is slightly matte, built from a vermilion or cinnabar layer over black that wears at the high points to expose a warm brown underlayer. Forms are simple discs, rounded rectangles and lobed trays with deep carved rims. The look is folk-craft, sculptural and handmade, with visible tool marks.
In the style of Iznik Ceramic Workshops, white quartz-frit ceramic painted under a brilliant clear glaze with vivid fresh colour. The characteristic palette is cobalt blue, turquoise, emerald green and a thick raised tomato-red, drawn crisply with black outlines on a bright white ground. Patterns are tulips, carnations, hyacinths, rosebuds, saz leaves, curling feathery leaves, and cloud bands arranged symmetrically in flowing arabesques. Tiles, plates, jugs and mosque lamps are glossy, with a crisp, fresh look and rhythmic, balanced repetition. Large wall panels form garden-like compositions around arches and borders.
In the style of Zildjian Cymbal Foundry, hand-hammered bronze cymbals with a bright warm gold sheen, circular and dished with a raised central bell. The surface shows concentric lathing grooves and dense dimpled hammer marks, with darker, burnished tones in the recesses and polished highlights on the ridges. Traditional versions look dark, brown and dull gold while newer versions are brilliantly polished and mirror bright. A small hole in the bell takes the strap, edges are thin and slightly wavy, and a printed stamp with an ornate logo, in black or gold ink, sits near the bell. Variations include flat rides, crash cymbals and hi-hats.
In the style of Tabriz Carpet Workshops, finely knotted wool and silk rugs with a central medallion on an open or dense field, framed by a wide main border and several narrow guard borders. Colours are rich and layered, with ivory, deep madder red, indigo, navy, soft turquoise and gold. Motifs include curling vines, palmettes, hunting scenes, trees of life, vases of flowers and cartouches, drawn in very precise, curved lines. The pile is short and velvety, with sharply defined details, strict symmetry and a spacious, courtly composition. Corner spandrels echo the medallion shape, and fringes are neat and white.
In the style of Kashan Carpet Workshops, closely knotted wool and silk carpets in a luxurious, deep colour scheme dominated by claret and ruby red, midnight blue and ivory, with a velvet-soft short pile. A large lobed central medallion with pendants floats on a field of dense scrolling vines, palmettes and arabesque blossoms, framed by a wide border with repeating floral cartouches. Fine detail, perfect symmetry and a slightly glossy sheen make them formal and elegant. Corners repeat quarter medallions, and tiny outlines in ivory define the stems and leaves.
In the style of Fes Zellij Workshops, mosaic panels of hand-cut glazed clay tiles inlaid into an interlocking geometric puzzle. Stars with eight, twelve or sixteen points radiate from the centre, with kite, hexagon and chevron pieces in vibrant glossy colours, namely cobalt blue, emerald green, saffron yellow, white, black, deep red-brown and turquoise. Fountains, walls and floors carry the repeating tessellations, and edges are crisp with thin white grout lines. Borders carry calligraphic bands or carved plaster, and surfaces sit below carved cedar and stucco. The result is a precise, endlessly repeating, jewel-like pattern.
In the style of Venini Murano Glass, hand-blown Venetian art glass in elegant simple shapes with thin walls and flowing asymmetric curves. Colours are translucent and luminous, including emerald green, amber, ruby, aquamarine, lilac and opaque milk white, sometimes layered with a clear casing. Techniques create fused patches of colour like woven fabric, delicate lace threads, vertical ribbons, bubbled inclusions and acid-etched satin surfaces. Vases, bowls, bottles and chandeliers feel light, sculptural and playful, with clean lines inspired by mid-century modernism. Many pieces are polished at the rim and carry a small etched mark.
In the style of Fortuny Textiles, printed velvet and cotton furnishing fabrics with densely stamped metallic pigment in gold and silver on deep, mottled backgrounds. Colours are rich and muted, including aubergine, indigo, burnt sienna, olive, ochre, antique rose and dusty teal, with a hazy, layered, hand-printed look. Patterns are Renaissance pomegranates, Islamic arabesques, Cretan and Persian stylised animals, ogee lattices and medallions repeating in tight rows. The surface has a faint shimmer from the metal ink, a soft aged glow and a velvety hand. Silk pleated gowns and lamps with a painted parchment shade complete the rich, theatrical, exotic mood.
In the style of Gobelins Manufactory, huge woven wool and silk wall tapestries that look like paintings, with dense hatching of finely graded coloured threads. Compositions are grand and theatrical, showing mythological and historical scenes, hunting parties, hanging garlands and architectural frames with heavy woven borders of acanthus, laurel, fruit, shields and gilt-looking scrollwork. Palette is deep and slightly aged, with crimson, royal blue, forest green, ochre, gold and warm brown, plus pale highlights. Figures are idealised and baroque in pose with billowing drapery and dramatic clouds, and the weave is tight, flat and smooth.
In the style of Aubusson Tapestry Workshops, flat-woven wool and silk tapestries and rugs with soft, pastel-leaning colour. The palette uses faded rose pink, powder blue, sage green, cream, pale gold and muted terracotta. Designs include verdure scenes of leafy forests with birds, large acanthus leaves, floral bouquets, pastoral figures and romantic medallions framed by woven ribbons, garlands and scrolling cartouches. The weave is flat with no pile and a slightly chalky, dusty, antique hand. Compositions feel airy, graceful, curved and symmetrical, in a Rococo and Neoclassical manner, with delicate cream borders.
In the style of Beauvais Tapestry Manufactory, woven tapestries and upholstery panels with a playful, pastoral Rococo character. Scenes show gallant shepherds, children at play, garden romances, exotic animals and fables, rendered in soft, sweet colours such as pink, cream, sky blue, pale green and gold with brown outlines. Frames of woven flowers, ribbons, scallops and C-shaped scrolls surround chair backs, screens and sofas. The weave is fine and smooth and the colours glow like a painted pastel. Mood is light, romantic and decorative, with plump forms and flowing curves.
In the style of Savonnerie Manufactory, thick, plush hand-knotted wool pile carpets with a velvet surface and a sculptural, monumental pattern. Layouts are formal and symmetrical, with large acanthus scrolls, floral bouquets, shells, cornucopia, garlands and architectural cartouches on a dark ground in deep blue, black, brown or crimson. Motifs are shaded to look three-dimensional, with ivory, gold, rose, sage green and yellow highlights. Borders are wide, with trompe-l oeil mouldings and corners holding fruit and flowers. The overall feeling is that of a royal palace floor, sumptuous, ordered and heavy.
In the style of Sevres Porcelain Manufactory, soft-paste and hard-paste porcelain with deep saturated ground colours, namely a rich royal blue, turquoise, a delicate rose pink and apple green, all heavily gilded. Reserved panels on vases, tea services and plates hold finely painted cherubs, pastoral figures, birds and flower bouquets in naturalistic colours. Gilt borders, scrolls, beading and lacy foliage trim every edge, and handles are sculpted into curving branches or flower forms. Forms are swelling, ornate and Rococo, with a glossy, perfectly smooth glaze. Biscuit figurines remain matte white, with silky, snow-like texture.
In the style of Royal Delft, tin-glazed earthenware with a creamy white ground and a vivid cobalt blue painted decoration. Scenes include windmills, canals, sailing ships, tulips, villagers, birds and landscapes drawn with fine brushwork and tonal shading. Plates, tiles, tall tulip vases and vases with pyramids of spouts feature curling scrollwork, floral borders and small figures. The glaze is shiny, slightly bluish-white and pooled in places, and the blue ranges from pale to near-black. Tile pictures in grids repeat simple blue motifs in corner spandrels. The mood is crisp, domestic, nostalgic and traditionally Dutch.
In the style of Whitefriars Glass, heavy, thick-walled studio-style glass with bold sculptural shapes and a textured surface. Famous items include bark-textured vases with irregular tree-like ridges, chunky bubbles and cased layers of strong colour. The palette is deep and earthy, with tangerine orange, meadow green, kingfisher blue, aubergine purple, ruby red, amber and smoky grey. Forms are cylindrical, tapered, faceted or swirled, with heavy bases and slight asymmetry. Moulded ribbed pattern and dimpled decoration create a strong tactile, glowing mid-century impression. Surfaces are glossy, with a deep inner colour.
In the style of Steuben Glass, crystal clear, flawless, heavy lead-free optical glass vases, bowls and sculptures with a pure glass transparency and thick, polished walls. Shapes are clean, simple and modernist, with crisp curves, tapered columns, deep facets and bowls resting on thick bases. Engraved or cut decoration is minimal, such as thin copper-wheel engraved animals, fish and plants, or sharp geometric cuts that refract light. Colour appears rarely, in subtle tinted tones such as pale gold or aqua. The mood is cool, luminous, precise and quiet, like a block of ice shaped by hand.
In the style of Gorham Silver, heavy sterling tableware, tea sets and trophies in a lavish late-Victorian and Art Nouveau manner. Surfaces are hand-hammered or chased, with bright polished highlights and darker oxidised recesses. Decoration includes embossed flowers, lily pads, whiplash curves, dragonflies, Japanese-inspired motifs, mixed metal inlays of copper and brass, and flowing organic handles. Teapots and bowls are rounded, with applied leaves and figural finials and ebonised or ivory-coloured insulators. Pieces have a rich, sculptural, ornamental feel and a characteristic martelled texture. The marks are small stamped symbols on the base.
In the style of Rookwood Pottery, glazed art pottery vases with a high-gloss, dripping or smooth glaze and painted decoration. The signature standard glaze has dark backgrounds blending from brown to amber, orange and olive green, with painted flowers, leaves, fruit and animals, such as nasturtiums or pansies, in a naturalistic, softly blurred manner. Later styles include matte glazes in soft green, blue and pink, as well as Art Deco forms with geometric and stylised flowers. Forms are rounded, with simple lines and a mix of tall vases and bowls. Marks on the base include a stamped flame-ringed emblem.
In the style of Roycroft Arts and Crafts Community, rectilinear oak furniture, hand-hammered copper and books made in a plain handmade manner. Furniture has square, sturdy legs, exposed through-tenons, flat slats and a warm medium-brown fumed finish, with visible grain. Copper bowls, lamps and bookends show hammer marks, rivets, strap hinges and a deep brown or warm penny-red patina. Books use thick handmade paper, rubricated initials, woodcut-like borders, heavy black type and limp suede or vellum covers with a stamped orb and cross emblem. The mood is simple, honest, rustic and monastic.
In the style of Ntonso Adinkra Cloth Makers, handwoven cotton cloth stamped with symbols using carved calabash stamps and a dark, glossy tree-bark dye. Rows of small, bold black motifs, such as concentric circles, spirals, crosses, ladders, hearts and stylised plants, repeat inside a grid divided by hand-drawn comb lines. Backgrounds are dyed deep red, brown, indigo, black or left natural cream, and mourning cloths are very dark. Symbols are slightly irregular, with inky edges, giving a handmade rhythm and graphic quality. The cloth is wrapped around the body like a toga and has stitched strips joined together.
In the style of Hudson Bay Company Point Blankets, thick, heavy wool blankets in a creamy off-white field with four horizontal stripes near each edge, in green, red, yellow and indigo blue. The stripes are bold, flat and evenly spaced, with the green and red dominating, and small black tick marks woven into one edge to show size. The cloth has a dense napped, felted surface that feels rustic, warm and hearty, with finished edges and no further decoration. Blankets are sold folded with a woven tag. Variants offer a sturdy single stripe, but the classic four-stripe look suggests trading posts, cabins and snow.
In the style of Ganado Trading Post Navajo Weavings, handwoven wool rugs with a deep Ganado red ground, black and white, and grey borders. A bold central diamond, often with stepped serrated edges or a cross motif, is placed in the centre, with smaller diamonds or hooks echoing in corners. Colours are earthy and limited, namely brick red, charcoal black, ivory and soft grey, with strong contrast. The border is a single frame with notches or crosses, and edges are neat. Weave is tight and flat with a slight handspun irregularity. Geometry is crisp, rectilinear and strongly symmetrical, with a warm, desert look.
In the style of Aldine Press, small portable books with a clean, balanced page, set in a crisp roman type with open, round letters and a slanted, narrow italic with joined strokes. Pages are uncluttered, with generous margins, justified text, simple initial capitals and almost no illustration. Title pages carry a small printer mark of a dolphin coiled around an anchor in black ink on cream paper. Bindings are soft vellum or brown leather with blind tooling, gilt edges and ties. The look is elegant, humanist, scholarly and compact, with an even, quiet, evenly inked colour on laid paper.
In the style of Plantin Press, dense, sturdy books of the Renaissance with well-spaced roman typefaces, italic accents and elaborate woodcut or copper-engraved title pages. Title pages are architectural, with columns, pediments, allegorical figures, garlands, cartouches and a printer emblem of a hand holding a compass. Pages show black ink on warm cream laid paper, with decorative headpieces, ornamented initials and neat marginal notes. Multi-language Bible volumes use columns in several scripts, symmetrical and orderly. Bindings are in tooled calf or vellum. The look is rich, rational, ornate and humanist.
In the style of Kelmscott Press, dense Gothic-revival books printed in heavy black blackletter type on thick handmade cream paper, with red ink for headings and marginal notes. Each page is framed by luxuriant woodcut borders of winding acanthus, vines, flowers, leaves and fruit in white on black, interlaced thickly. Large decorative initials in foliage set against dark ground open each chapter. Text blocks are tightly justified, with minimal leading and wide margins, giving a dark and ornate texture. Bindings use limp vellum with silk ties or half holland with boards. The mood is medieval, rich and romantic.
In the style of Doves Press, austere and elegant books with no illustration and no border. Pages are printed in a single classic roman type in rich black ink on thick cream handmade paper, with even word spacing, generous margins and a perfectly balanced column. Only coloured initials in red or blue, drawn with flowing calligraphic flourishes, add ornament, and chapter openings use large letters with simple lines. Title pages are plain with tidy letter spacing. Bindings are tooled with a simple gilt line pattern on limp vellum or morocco. The mood is pure, calm, disciplined and typographic.
In the style of Elzevir Press, tiny pocket-sized duodecimo books with neat, crisp roman and italic text set in narrow columns on small pages. The title page has a copper-engraved frame or a small printer device of a tree wound with a vine and an old man beside it. Typography is compact and clear, with compact page numbers, ornamental headpieces of printers flowers and small decorated initials. Paper is white to cream, and bindings are plain vellum or brown calf with gilt tooling and small marbled endpapers. The mood is compact, practical, tidy and scholarly.
In the style of Monotype Corporation, printed matter and specimen sheets showing carefully revived classical roman typefaces and crisp new classics. Text is set in fine serif with high contrast, bracketed serifs, balanced proportions and even colour, along with a few geometric sans-serif and slab designs. Specimen pages are neat, black ink on cream paper, with rows of alphabets in graduated sizes, small numbers and ornamental rules. Layouts use centred headings, small caps, letter-spaced capitals and discreet fleurons. The overall look is orderly, refined, classical and typographic, in the manner of a well-printed British book or newspaper.
In the style of Letraset, dry-transfer lettering sheets with rows of black or coloured typefaces printed on translucent sheets, with a waxy, slightly cracked appearance when rubbed down. Typeface samples are bold, expressive 1960s and 70s display faces, including chunky rounded letters, psychedelic swirls, inline shadows, stencil cuts, art-nouveau revival and space-age geometric forms. Sheets are laid out in a neat grid with tiny alphabets and punctuation, along with registration marks and a blue instruction grid. Words applied to paper are slightly uneven, with tiny gaps and imperfect baseline. The mood is cut-and-paste, punk poster and retro graphic design.
In the style of Rotring, precision technical drawing pens and mechanical pencils with a slim, hexagonal or round metal barrel in matte black or silver, with a knurled grip and a red ring around the top. The shape is industrial and tool-like, with a long conical metal nose, a retractable sleeve, a clip and tiny printed line-width numbers in white on the barrel. Colours are black, brushed chrome and a vivid red accent. Technical pens use colour-coded end caps and ink cartridges. Finishes are crisp and engineered, and the mood is draughtsman, architect studio and exacting accuracy.
In the style of Staedtler, drawing pencils and erasers in a distinctive scheme. Wooden pencils have a hexagonal body lacquered in bright blue and black bands with a vivid yellow upper section or a navy and red set, and a gold-printed or white type. Erasers are white, wrapped in blue paper sleeves with a black and red print. Technical pens and leads come in clean tin boxes or plastic cases, coloured in blue, black and white, with a simple sans-serif type. The look is functional, German, tidy and classroom-like, with tidy contrast bands and a flat, smooth lacquer.
In the style of Faber-Castell, pencils and colouring sets with a deep dark-green lacquer finish and gold hot-foil stamped lettering and a double-knight emblem. The barrels are hexagonal or round, with a smoothly dipped end in colour and fine grading marks. Tins and boxes are dark green, dark brown or black with gold lettering, with rows of pencils and pastels aligned in gradients. Luxury writing tools come in polished wood with silver or platinum-plated fittings and knurled details. Mood is classic, scholarly, European and craftsmanlike.
In the style of Pelikan, piston-filling fountain pens with a plump cigar-shaped barrel and cap in celluloid or resin. Classic models have a black cap, a translucent ink window and a green striped, tortoiseshell or black body with gold-coloured trim bands. The cap clip is a slender arrow or a bird-shaped tail, and a gold nib bears fine engraved scrollwork. Later versions appear in bright blue, red and white stripes, along with a rounded, thick body. Inks and boxes use deep green and white, with a pelican and chicks emblem. The look is tidy, German, sturdy and luminous.
In the style of Montblanc, massive cigar-shaped fountain pens in polished black precious resin with gold-plated rings, a clip and a large two-tone gold nib. The cap is topped with a white six-pointed star shaped like a snow-capped peak, rounded with a domed shape. Barrels are glossy and deep black, with thin gold rings and engraved numbers. Special editions use striped, marbled or burgundy resin. Cases are black leather with a gold emblem, and notebooks and cufflinks follow the same dark and gold luxury. The mood is stately, confident, executive and understated.
In the style of Parker Pens, streamlined fountain pens and ballpoints with a cylindrical, slightly tapered body. Barrels come in glossy burgundy, blue, black or grey with a fine herringbone, vertical grooves or smooth finish. Caps are brushed stainless steel or gold-filled, with a ribbed texture, and a distinctive arrow-shaped clip that stands proud from the cap. A hooded nib is covered by a smooth tapered shield. Trim is thin chrome or gold bands. The mood is mid-century post-war optimism, with sleek, bullet-like forms and good engineering.
In the style of Waterman Pens, elegant fountain pens with slim, rounded barrels and caps in black hard rubber, red ripple pattern, or sterling silver filigree overlay. Ripple designs swirl in mottled red and black, and metal overlays show Art Deco geometric grooves, basketweave and lacy filigree. Pens have thin gold-filled bands, a lever filler on the side, a gently arched clip and a flexible gold nib with fine ornament. Colours are warm, rich and aged, with an orange-red sheen. Boxes are lined with velvet. The mood is refined, Edwardian and Art Deco.
In the style of Sheaffer Pens, bulbous, torpedo-shaped fountain pens in glossy striped celluloid colours, such as pearl and jade green, marine black, grey, burgundy and blue. The cap has a thin white-dot emblem on top and a slim chrome or gold trim ring. A distinctive inlaid white dot sits at the top of the cap, and a long nib is gold with a triangular or hooded shape. Clips are straight, flat and sleek, with a spear or tapering form. Barrels are smooth, rounded, streamlined and glossy, in an Art Deco and early jet-age manner.
In the style of Pilot Namiki Maki-e Pens, slim lacquered fountain pens with a glossy black or red urushi ground decorated with delicate gold, silver and coloured powder. Designs show tiny cranes, dragonflies, bamboo, plum blossom, waves and mountains in raised, sparkling gold with soft gradations, often covering the whole barrel and cap. The metal nib is gold, with fine engraved scrolls, and fittings are gold or rhodium. The forms are smooth and elegant, with rounded ends. The pen comes in a box of paulownia wood. The mood is quiet luxury, patient craft and nature-inspired.
In the style of Uni Mitsubishi Pencil, premium graphite pencils with a deep, mirror-like glossy paint finish in dark burgundy or black, with a single gold foil stamped line of tiny type and a slim gold band. Hexagonal barrels are smooth and perfectly even, ending in a clean flat end with a thin ferrule. Boxes are slim and rectangular, with a window, in dark maroon, black or silver. Pens such as pure-grey capped markers appear in clear transparent bodies with coloured grips. The overall mood is quiet, luxurious, Japanese, simple and meticulously made.
In the style of Lamy, modernist pens with a simple, functional design in matte black plastic and brushed stainless steel. Barrels are straight cylinders with a faceted triangular grip section, and a thin sprung wire clip extends along the cap as a single flat bar. Surfaces are matte and fine-grained, with only small dots or ink windows. Colours are black, charcoal grey, white, and later bright red, yellow and blue. The tip of the nib is silvery steel, clean and plain. Boxes are minimal. The mood is German industrial design, school-like and elegantly austere.
In the style of Cross Pens, slim cylindrical pens and pencils with a clean, classical shape and a smooth finish in chrome, brushed gold-filled or lacquer. The barrel carries a tight diamond-patterned engraving or fine ridged stripes and a straight narrow clip with a tiny ball tip. Rings are thin and the profile is a perfectly even tube with a rounded cap that twists at the top. Colours are silver, gold, black and deep blue. The cases are leather or velvet lined. The mood is business-like, classic, restrained, and subtly formal.
In the style of Winsor and Newton, artists colours in small metal tubes and wooden boxes with a classic, scholarly look. Tubes are silver or coloured foil with black or white lettering, a cream label and a bold red or black band, with the colour name and swatch. Mahogany or black japanned tin watercolour boxes hold rows of half pans in brilliant pigment, with a lift-out tray and a brass catch. Brushes have long, tapered wooden handles in red, black or natural, with a gleaming crimped ferrule. Designs use dense serif type and ornate Victorian borders, giving a studious and traditional feeling.
In the style of Caran d Ache, coloured pencils and crayons in metal tins with a vivid, saturated and carefully graded spectrum. Pencils are hexagonal with a glossy deep colour matching the lead, a white or gold imprint and a smooth round end dipped in the same colour. Tins are slim, in silver, red or royal blue with a hinged lid, neat rows and a bright mountain or lettering emblem. Writing instruments include hexagonal metal pens in glossy lacquer with a crisp chiselled look. Colours are intense, with strong reds, cyans and yellows, and a pristine, orderly, Swiss feel.
In the style of Steinway and Sons, concert grand pianos in a glossy black polished lacquer, with a mirror-like curved rim, sweeping bentside and a large raised lid. The legs are sturdy, round and tapered, ending in brass casters, with a lyre-shaped pedal assembly in gold-toned metal beneath. A gilt cast-iron plate shows within, the name is in gold lettering on the fallboard and keys are white with ebony sharps. Decoration is elegant and restrained. Alternate finishes include dark mahogany or burl walnut. The mood is grand, opulent, formal and concert-hall dignified.
In the style of Bosendorfer, Viennese concert grand pianos with a distinctive case in glossy black or rich warm wood, such as walnut, cherry or maple, with a longer, extended bass end of extra black keys. The rim is slender and curved, with a refined and quiet profile. Legs are carved and tapering, and the music desk is lace-like with a shallow ornate shape. Edging is thin, and trim in gold or brass is tasteful. Some models have elaborate inlay, gilt carving and painted panels in the Baroque and Secession manner. The mood is aristocratic, warm, ornate and old-world.
In the style of Selmer Paris, saxophones and clarinets with an elegant curved brass body finished in lacquer, with a warm honey-gold hue, or silver plating. Keys are neatly arranged, pearly rounded buttons with fine engraving on the bell showing swirling floral scrollwork in the Art Deco manner. The neck is long and gently bent, and bells are wide and flared. Tone-hole rims are finely drawn and key guards are tidy. Cases are black and plush-lined. The mood is jazz-age, Parisian, tidy and rich in warm gold.
In the style of Taylor Guitars, acoustic guitars with a modern, clean, sleek look. Bodies are slim with a cutaway, bright natural-coloured spruce tops, and sides and back in sapele, rosewood or koa with fine grain. Finishes are thin, glossy and flawless, and the edges have rounded bevels, especially at the lower arm. Inlays are minimal, such as slim abalone or ebony markers in an angular, elegant shape, with thin bindings and a rosette ring of dark inlay. Headstocks have a tapered, smooth curve with an engraved logo. The mood is precise, bright, contemporary, and carefully finished.
In the style of Martin Guitars, classic dreadnought acoustic guitars with a large, rounded rectangular body, a warm golden spruce top that ages to amber, and rosewood or mahogany sides and back. Bindings are cream or ivoroid and dark herringbone purfling rings the top. The soundhole is circled with fine rosette rings, and the pickguard is tortoiseshell. Fretboards are dark ebony with simple pearl dots or snowflake inlays on premium models. The headstock is squared with an inlaid pearl logo. The finish is satin or gloss. The mood is traditional, folksy and rootsy.
In the style of Gretsch Guitars, large hollow-body electric guitars with a flamboyant orange or deep red-brown lacquer finish, a gold-plated pickguard and bold decoration. Details include twin cutaways, gold sparkle or white binding, a G-shaped brand cutout on the body, a large V-shaped tailpiece in gold, and a tall headstock with a wide shape. Hardware is flashy, with chrome, gold, thumbscrew plates and tall knobs. Pickups sit in gold-plated surrounds. Alternate finishes include white falcon pearl, two-tone green and sparkle. The mood is rock and roll, showy, rockabilly and retro.
In the style of Rickenbacker, electric guitars and basses with a distinctive thin semi-acoustic body, with a glossy red, fireglo sunburst, jetglo black, mapleglo natural or white finish and white plastic binding. Features include a slotted headstock, a sharply pointed cutaway horn on one side, a stylish chrome bridge cover, triangular fretboard inlays, a chrome toaster-shaped pickup and a white pickguard with a tapered contour. Some models show a rounded cutaway with a curvy body edge and a rear control panel with tiny knobs. The mood is jangly, mod, 1960s British Invasion and psychedelic.
In the style of Bechstein, concert and salon grand pianos in glossy ebony black, with a classic Germanic design. Curved cases have a sharply defined rim, bold Jugendstil carved legs or plain tapering legs, and a gold-leaf painted decoration on historic art cases showing Neo-classical or Art Nouveau scenes in muted colours. The lid is large and the music desk is carved. Gold-toned lettering marks the fallboard. Alternative woods include rosewood, walnut and burr maple with warm mellow tones. The mood is cultured, stately, ornate, and drawing-room elegant.
In the style of Yamaha Pianos, grand and upright pianos in a flawless gloss black polyester finish, with smooth, crisp straight lines and uniform surfaces. Grand pianos have a simple curved rim, a clean music desk, square or rounded legs and a gold or silver fallboard emblem with three crossed tuning forks. Uprights are boxy, with neat panels, a fold-down lid and plain brass pedals. Alternative finishes include satin ebony, polished mahogany, walnut and white. The mood is modern, tidy, dependable, schoolroom and conservatoire, with perfect reflections.
In the style of Bauhaus Weimar Workshops, expressionist and early constructivist objects made in wood, metal, glass and textile. Furniture is built from stacked cubes, slats and planes in primary red, yellow, blue, black and white, with bold geometric shapes such as circle, square and triangle. Tapestries are abstract compositions of rectangles and stripes in saturated, earthy reds, blues, ochres and black. Ceramics and teapots are rounded and cylindrical in nickel or plain glaze. Posters use heavy sans-serif lettering, stacked on grids with diagonal bars. The mood is utopian, experimental, geometric and artisan.
In the style of Vkhutemas Workshops, constructivist posters, furniture and architectural models in stark geometry. Colours are red, black, white and cream, with occasional yellow and grey. Compositions place diagonals, circles, wedges and sharp bars at dynamic angles, with heavy angular sans-serif lettering and photomontage. Furniture is made of thin plywood, tubing and simple planes, folded or foldable with exposed joints. Architectural models show stacked boxes, cantilevers, cylinders and glass walls. Prints are flat and high-contrast, with rough printed textures. The mood is revolutionary, industrial, energetic and abstract.
In the style of De Stijl Workshops, furniture, posters and interiors built from straight horizontal and vertical lines and rectangles. Palette is limited to pure red, yellow and blue, together with white, black and grey, set in flat colour blocks with thick black dividing lines. Chairs are assembled from slim rails and flat panels that overlap and project at their ends, painted in contrasting colours with exposed ends. Rooms show asymmetrical balance and open planes. Lettering is blocky and constructed from squares. The mood is strict, rational, abstract and playful in its colour.
In the style of Omega Workshops, hand-painted furniture, ceramics and textiles with bold, loose, post-impressionist decoration. Brush marks are visible, with abstract blobs, stripes, spirals and stylised flowers or figures in strong colours such as mustard, magenta, turquoise, orange, lilac, green and black. Fabrics and screens use irregular, asymmetrical compositions with an unfinished hand-drawn quality. Tables and chairs are plain and painted all over in dense patterns. Pottery has a thick opaque glaze with spots and swirls. The mood is cheerful, avant-garde, amateurish by design and bohemian.
In the style of Guild of Handicraft, Arts and Crafts silver, enamel and jewellery in a hand-wrought manner. Hammered silver bowls, tankards and jewellery show visible tool marks, curved handles, wire twists and loose, flowing Art Nouveau lines. Peacocks, ships, tendrils, stylised flowers and hearts are set with cabochon moonstones, opals, turquoise or blister pearls and translucent coloured enamel in green, blue and rose. Items look soft and slightly irregular, with a matte, pale silver surface and delicate suspended drops. The mood is romantic, medieval-influenced, simple and craftsmanlike.
In the style of Shaker Mount Lebanon, plain and sturdy furniture and objects made from pine, maple and cherry with no ornament. Chairs have slender turned posts, ladder backs with arching slats, woven tape or rush seats and tapered legs. Tables, cupboards and drawers show clean flat panels, simple pegs, finger joints, wooden knobs and a smooth surface. Colours are muted milk-paint tones, namely barn red, mustard yellow, olive green, blue and the natural honey wood. Oval pantry boxes use swallowtail joints and copper tacks. Rooms have pegboards that hold chairs on the wall. The mood is serene, orderly, utilitarian and pure.
In the style of Amish Quilt Workshops, quilts made with large simple geometric blocks of solid-colour wool or cotton, in deep, saturated tones. Typical palette includes black, plum purple, royal blue, forest green, wine red, brown and dusty rose, with no printed patterns. Designs include a centre diamond, bars, sunshine and shadow, and nine-patch squares, framed by wide borders and bound in contrasting colour. Fine, dense quilting stitches create feathers, wreaths, cables and grids in the dark areas, giving a subtle relief. The mood is bold, austere, modern-looking and graphic.
In the style of Gzhel Porcelain, white glazed porcelain and earthenware painted only in cobalt blue brushwork, in graduated tones from pale sky blue to deep navy. Decorations are floral sprigs, leaves, swirling petals, birds, scalloped rims and simple landscapes, painted freely with a wide brush in a single stroke style. Forms include teapots, vases, figurines of animals, tea sets and samovar-like shapes, with rounded and plump outlines. The white ground is bright and glossy, and the blue appears washed and flowing. The mood is folk-like, cheerful, fresh and quintessentially Russian.
In the style of Lomonosov Imperial Porcelain Factory, bright white porcelain in two manners. The imperial manner shows gilded rims, cobalt blue nets, a mesh pattern of dark blue lattice with gold dots and rich floral reserves in naturalistic colours. The revolutionary manner of the 1920s shows suprematist and propaganda designs with sharp red, black and white geometry, hammers, stars, workers and slogans in stencilled Cyrillic lettering. Figurines are lively and glossy. Forms are fine, thin and delicate in the imperial style, and flat plates in the later style are filled by boldly angular designs.
In the style of Palekh Lacquer Miniatures, black glossy papier-mache boxes, brooches and panels painted in egg tempera with fairy tale scenes. Elongated, elegant figures with small heads and graceful curved poses walk through stylised hills, towers, trees and onion domes. Palette is bright red, gold, emerald green, white and orange against deep lustrous black, with fine gold highlights outlining draperies and ornamental borders of tiny patterns. The inside lid is red lacquer. Details are miniature and precise, as in an icon, and the mood is mythical, jewel-like and storybook.
In the style of Talavera Uriarte, tin-glazed earthenware with a thick, glossy milky white glaze and hand-painted decoration in cobalt blue, with accents of yellow, green, orange, black and sometimes turquoise. Patterns are flowers, vines, leaves, birds, rosettes and geometric borders, painted freely with a loose touch and a slightly raised, shiny paint. Plates, jars, jugs, basins and wall tiles are rounded and weighty, with visible pinholes and small unevenness in the glaze. Tiles combine into large courtyard panels in blue and white. The mood is Mexican colonial, rustic, festive and handmade.
In the style of Hereke Imperial Carpet Factory, exceptionally fine silk and wool carpets with a very high knot density, glossy silk pile and sharp, minute detail. Designs show central medallions, prayer arches, Ottoman tulips, carnations, hyacinths, curling saz leaves and rosettes in a courtly arrangement, with metal thread in gold and silver for added shimmer. Colours are ivory, turquoise, rose, deep sapphire, emerald and ruby. Borders are narrow and multiple, with perfectly even geometry and soft elegant curves. The mood is imperial, lustrous, exquisite and delicate.
In the style of Batik Danar Hadi, Javanese hand-drawn wax-resist cotton and silk cloths in earthy palette. Colours include soga brown, indigo blue, cream and black, with occasional red and gold. Patterns are classical, such as diagonal parang bands of interlocking S shapes, kawung circles in a quatrefoil grid, truntum small flowers, and sido mukti cartouches with birds and wings. Fine dotted lines and wavering white outlines from hand-applied wax form the delicate work. Cloth shows crackle veins where the wax cracked, and tonal depth. The mood is courtly, rhythmic, refined and heritage-rich.
In the style of Bat Trang Ceramic Kilns, Vietnamese stoneware and porcelain in cream-white glaze with blue underglaze painting, as well as brown, green and iron-brown glazes. Decoration shows lotus flowers, chrysanthemums, dragons, bamboo, birds, wave scrolls and geometric key-fret borders, brushed in free, flowing cobalt blue or incised in clay. Forms are tall vases with swelling shoulders, jars with lids, bowls, plates and teapots, with thick walls and a slightly uneven hand-thrown quality. The crackled ivory glaze is soft and subtle, and the mood is gentle, rustic, and unpretentious.
In the style of New Zealand Maori Arts and Crafts Institute, ornately carved wooden meeting house posts, panels and canoe prows covered in dense curved relief. Figures have large heads with wide eyes inlaid with paua shell, protruding tongues, three-fingered hands and bodies decorated with spirals, interlocked koru curves and notched ridges. Wood is dark, often totara, polished brown, with red ochre pigment and details of white and black. Lattice panels and woven reed work, such as tukutuku panels in diagonal patterns, fill the spaces between the posts. The mood is ancestral, powerful, sculptural and rhythmic, with continuous flowing curves.
In the style of Thonet, bentwood furniture made from steam-bent solid beech rods formed into continuous flowing curves. Slender round-section frames, looping circles and S-scrolls in the backrest, a round or oval seat of woven cane or pressed plywood with tiny perforations, splayed delicate legs joined by a ring brace. Finish is stained dark mahogany brown, walnut or ebonised black with a satin lacquer, with visible screw heads and metal collars at joints. Cafe chairs, rocking chairs and hat stands, light, airy and sculptural, mass-produced Viennese coffeehouse mood, later chrome tubular steel cantilever chairs with cane panels and black leather in the 1920s.
In the style of Herman Miller, American mid-century modern furniture with molded plywood and fibreglass shells in organic curves, perched on thin wire struts or splayed wooden dowel legs with metal cross braces. Colours are warm walnut, ash and rosewood veneer, black leather with plump cushions, and bright pops of orange, mustard yellow, turquoise and white. Lounge chairs with stacked cushioned panels on a cast aluminium swivel base, bubble-shaped sofas, slatted platform benches, egg-shaped and kidney-shaped forms, exposed joints and brass or chrome hardware. Playful, optimistic, sunlit California living room and office look, clean honest construction, later ergonomic mesh task chairs in graphite with visible skeletal frames.
In the style of Knoll, high modernist furniture with polished chrome-plated flat steel bars bent in precise cantilever arcs, supporting thin black or tan leather sling seats and tufted cushions held by visible buttons and straps. Tulip-shaped pedestals with a single white moulded base and a round white marble or laminate tabletop, bent wire diamond-shaped chairs, glass-topped coffee tables on chrome X-frames, sculpted rosewood sideboards. Palette of white, black, chrome, cream and saturated primary upholstery in wool tweed. Minimal, architectural, boardroom and corporate lobby elegance, pared down to structure with sharp crisp edges and mirror shine.
In the style of Vitra, contemporary design furniture with sculptural moulded polypropylene and plastic shells in matte pastel and primary colours, thin steel rod legs or wooden dowel bases, and playful oversized forms like gnome-shaped stools and spidery floor lamps. Curvy lounge chairs with wrapped upholstery in felt, wool and leather, black ash and oak veneer, white lacquer. Reissued mid-century plywood and fibreglass classics sit beside deconstructed, geometric, exhibition-like pieces. Crisp Swiss and German gallery aesthetic, witty, colourful, clean shadow lines, studio product photography on seamless white backdrops.
In the style of Artek, Finnish modernist furniture of pale laminated birch with a distinctive bent L-shaped leg that curls into a smooth scroll beneath the seat. Round three-legged stools, simple tables, cantilevered chairs with lightly curved plywood seats, and hanging pendant lamps. Natural birch, white lacquer, black linoleum tabletops and colourful lacquered legs in red, yellow, blue and green, woven webbing and canvas upholstery in stripes. Warm, humane, Scandinavian functionalism, soft rounded edges, light blond wood grain, nordic apartment and school hall mood.
In the style of Fritz Hansen, Danish modern furniture with sculptural moulded laminated wood shells in a curvy ant, butterfly or egg silhouette, pinched waist and no visible join between seat and back. Slim chromed steel tube legs on three or four points, upholstery in tight leather or tweed wool, coloured lacquered veneer in black, white, lime green, deep red and warm walnut. Swivel pod chairs on a spiderlike cast aluminium base, generous cushioning, glossy and smooth surfaces. Elegant, airy, hotel lobby and Copenhagen design office feel with smooth organic flow, never fussy.
In the style of Carl Hansen, Danish cabinetmaker furniture in solid oak, teak and walnut with soaked, oiled or soaped finishes showing fine grain. Curved wishbone-shaped backrests with a Y-shaped steam-bent top rail, hand-woven natural paper cord seats, tapered rounded legs, finely crafted pegged joints and visible wood joinery. Low lounge chairs with leather straps, sculptural oak armrests that float, round-edged dining tables. Palette of honey, pale blond, soft brown and cream leather. Calm, tactile, handcrafted, minimalist Scandinavian dining room and living room.
In the style of IKEA, flat-pack Scandinavian home furnishings in pale birch, pine and white-painted particleboard, with simple rectangular boxes, slim peg or tapered legs and rounded corners. Palette of white, light wood, bright cobalt blue and sunshine yellow, with muted greys, sage and pastel accents in textiles.
