Typography mixes decorative Victorian slab letters and fine engraved text in an orange, black and cream palette. The look is old English pantry heritage, with a fussy, crowded label and a reassuringly aged feel.
In the style of Marmite, a squat, round-shouldered glass jar with a broad base and a yellow screw lid, containing a thick, dark brown spread. A bold yellow label with a shiny surface and a dark navy-blue or black band, with a white scrolled wordmark in chunky serif capitals. Round and stout, like a small cauldron, with ridges at the shoulder and a wide mouth. Palette of bright yellow, deep red or brown, black and white. The look is blunt, comic, recognisable, with a squat jar silhouette and a strong two-tone label that shouts from the shelf.
In the style of Heinz, a tall, narrow glass ketchup bottle with a long neck, faceted glass shoulders, a metal screw cap, and a keystone-shaped label in cream with a red tomato-red border. A vertical stripe of bold red along the label with white serif lettering, and a small gherkin-pickle emblem. Cans and jars of beans with the same red-and-white label and a vivid blue stripe. Palette of tomato red, cream, gold and blue. Type is a mix of ornate serif capitals, banner ribbons and fine print, suggesting a heritage American pantry and a tidy, symmetrical layout.
In the style of Campbell, a straight cylindrical tin can with a bright red upper two-thirds and a white lower third, divided by a thin gold band. A gold medallion seal with embossed scalloped edges sits at the centre, flanked by ornamental elements. Lettering is in a thin red script of connected loops for the main mark, with white sans-serif capitals for the flavour name on the red field. Plain, flat, symmetrical, with a pale cream label and a ribbed can lid. The red-and-white palette gives a grocery shelf an instantly legible tin-can pattern, repeating as a grid.
In the style of Tabasco, a tiny, slim 5-ounce glass bottle with a long neck, a small rounded base and a flat shoulder, filled with a vivid red-orange pepper sauce. A green plastic screw cap with a tiny dropper insert, an oval-shaped paper neck label in deep green, a red diamond-shaped central label with the name in white serif capitals, and a thin gold border. Coloured caps vary by flavour. Palette of fire-engine red, forest green and cream. The look is slim, hand-labeled, heritage-Louisiana, with a diamond mark on a narrow bottle.
In the style of Moleskine, a pocket or A5 notebook with a hard cover wrapped in black matte oilcloth-like material with rounded corners, a cloth bookmark ribbon, an elastic band closure, and an inner pocket in cardboard at the back. Cream-ivory acid-free paper, ruled or plain, with a sewn spine and a rounded corner finish. A small black-and-white paper band wraps the outside with a text insert. Sober, minimal, black on cream. Thin sans-serif text, no embellishments, and an understated manufacturing detail. The look is urban, intellectual, travel-worn and elegant.
In the style of Aesop, amber brown glass or matte brown plastic bottles and tubes with plain cream-white paper labels, set in neat black type in a thin, clean serif such as a classical roman with a long detailed ingredients list below. Pump dispensers in black or brass, squat apothecary jars, and aluminium tubes in muted tones. Stores have raw timber shelves, concrete floors, brass sinks, stacked brown bottles and dim warm lighting. Palette of amber brown, cream, black and grey-green. The look is literate, apothecary-inspired, restrained, tactile, minimal and quietly luxurious.
In the style of Lush, handmade soaps cut in large hunks, bath bombs and shampoo bars displayed naked on wooden shelves and wicker baskets, coloured in vivid pinks, greens, purples and blues with swirling marbled patterns. Stores are open-fronted with green and black decor, chalkboards and exposed wood. The scent drifts outside. Look is hand-made, playful, messy, with hand-lettered brush type and kraft wrapping, bright and intense.
In the style of The Body Shop, forest-green painted shop fronts and shelves, with a glossy dark green and white label, plain plastic bottles with a simple matt coloured cap and thin white typography. A forest-green painted panel with white lettering runs across the signboard. Natural ingredients such as shea, cocoa and tea tree are represented by small simple illustrations. The look is earnest, earthy, ethical, with a green and white palette and plain utilitarian containers.

In the style of Braun, calm functional German industrial design of the 1950s to 1970s. Plain rectangular boxes with softly radiused corners, matte satin-finish sheet metal or textured plastic in off-white, pale grey, black and graphite. Controls are small round dials, short slide switches and flush push buttons, grouped in neat grids and set in a ruled layout. Tiny accents of orange, yellow or green mark the key control. Lettering is lowercase or clean grotesque sans-serif, thin and evenly spaced, printed in black or white on the face. Speaker grilles are fine perforated or slotted aluminium. Shavers, clocks, radios, record players and kitchen blenders share this quiet, honest, ornament-free look with generous empty space and precise tolerances.
In the style of Apple, minimalist consumer electronics with seamless slab forms. Thin rectangles with uniformly rounded corners and continuous tight radii, milled aluminium bodies in silver, space grey, gold and soft pastel anodised colours, edge-to-edge glass fronts with thin black bezels. Glossy white polycarbonate in the early 2000s, then brushed metal unibody construction. No visible screws, almost no buttons, only a few precisely cut slim keys, circular camera lenses grouped on a glass back, and a small polished logo silhouette of a bitten fruit centred on the rear. Interfaces use clean sans-serif type, generous white space, soft translucent blur layers and glossy rounded icons. Packaging is white and sparse. Mood is clean, premium and calm.
In the style of Sony, Japanese consumer electronics of the 1980s and 1990s. Compact black or silver boxes with beveled edges, brushed aluminium faceplates and dense rows of small rectangular rubber or plastic buttons. Portable cassette players in metallic blue and silver with orange accents, Trinitron televisions with deep black cabinets and flat-sided tubes, mini hi-fi stacks with tiny green and amber LED level meters and blue-green vacuum fluorescent displays. Fine stencil-like capital sans-serif labels, thin embossed model numbers, small rounded corners and engineered, high-tech miniaturised proportions. Later years bring minimalist matte black slabs with glowing coloured lighting strips. Mood is precise, technical and aspirational.
In the style of Technics, 1970s and 1980s Japanese hi-fi separates. Wide flat black or silver components with thick brushed aluminium front panels, heavy rotary knobs with knurled edges, rows of slim piano-style push keys, and rectangular illuminated meters with needle gauges or segmented green and amber LEDs. Direct-drive turntables with a flat rectangular plinth in black or silver, a strobe-dotted platter ring, a slim tonearm and a large pitch slider with finger-sized fader. Neat fine white sans-serif lettering, small engraved scale lines, rack-mount proportions and glossy dark smoked acrylic lids. Mood is serious, studio-grade and quietly luxurious.
In the style of Nintendo, playful Japanese game hardware. Compact rounded rectangles with chunky friendly proportions, glossy and matte plastic in white, light grey, red, black and bright primary or pastel colours. Controllers have a cross-shaped directional pad, small round coloured face buttons in red, blue, yellow or green, and short slim start keys, all set in tidy symmetrical layouts. The 1980s look has a cream-grey case with dark red trim and gold-lettered labels, boxy cartridges with ridged grips and rectangular stickers. Later devices use clean white or black slab shells, thin colourful detachable controllers, and rounded sans-serif logo lettering in red. Mood is cheerful, approachable and toy-like but neatly built.
In the style of Sega, 1990s Japanese game consoles and arcade hardware. Glossy black plastic shells with sharp angular wedge shapes and circular recessed cartridge slots, moulded ridges and vents, curved controller grips with three or six oversized round buttons and a round directional disc. Cobalt blue lettering and swirl logos, white and orange accents, loud saturated cabinet artwork with bold outlines and hard-edged speed lines, and glowing red or green power LEDs. Dreamcast-era units are pale off-white and square with a soft spiral on the lid. Typography is slanted bold italic capitals with a futuristic, fast feel. Mood is edgy, energetic and arcade-bright.
In the style of Atari, late 1970s and early 1980s American home game consoles and computers. Heavy wedge-shaped boxes clad in simulated dark woodgrain vinyl on the front with black ribbed plastic tops, chunky silver toggle switches in a row along the top edge, and thick black joysticks with a single round red button on a square base. Warm brown, black and silver palette. Cartridges are black with ridged grips and brightly illustrated paper labels showing painted spacescapes in orange, purple and teal. Logo is a stylised three-bar fuji-like trident shape in white or red with rounded geometric capitals. Computers are beige-grey with chunky keys. Mood is warm, retro-futuristic, 1970s den.
In the style of Commodore, 1980s home computers. Wide beige or brown-beige plastic keyboard-case computers with a sloped wedge profile, thick rows of chunky rectangular keys with grey and light-brown caps carrying coloured graphic symbols on their fronts, a rainbow-coloured stripe or slim dark band across the case, tiny red power LEDs and a ribbed vent grille. Matching cream-coloured floppy disk drives and cassette decks, joysticks with black bases and red fire buttons, and boxy CRT monitors with rounded dark bezels. Interfaces show a blue screen with a light-blue border and blocky pixel text. Logo is a bold angular chevron mark in stylised capitals. Mood is nerdy, hobbyist and cosy.
In the style of Olivetti, Italian office machines of the 1950s to 1970s treated as sculpture. Typewriters in bright signal red, lemon yellow, teal and orange glossy moulded plastic or pressed steel with soft flowing organic curves, rounded keyboards with white round-topped keys, and compact carry-case shells in matching colours. Calculators and computer cabinets have smooth enamelled panels, recessed rows of tiny coloured keys and small round dials. Lettering is lower-case or rounded sans-serif in white or black, often a small stripe of colour on the face. Mood is playful, elegant, optimistic and artisanal, with simple shapes polished to a gleaming industrial finish.
In the style of Bang & Olufsen, Danish luxury audio and television design. Slender, tall, minimalist forms in brushed and polished aluminium, black glass and pale oiled wood, with thin plates floating in space, tapered pyramid or cone speakers, and fabric-covered grilles in charcoal or warm grey. Controls are almost invisible, with hairline touch strips, a single large circular dial, a slim slide-down panel hiding hidden buttons, and subtle white or amber displays. Edges are razor-sharp with tiny perfectly even gaps. Palette is silver, black, natural oak, dark walnut and occasional soft earthy tones. Lettering is tiny, thin and lowercase. Mood is refined, sculptural and serene.
In the style of Leica, German rangefinder cameras. Compact rectangular bodies with a flat top plate, black enamel paint or polished silver chrome, wrapped in finely pebbled black vulcanite leatherette. A small viewfinder window and coupled range window sit at the top front, with a knurled shutter-speed dial, a thin film-advance lever and a round rewind knob on top. Lenses are slim black or chrome barrels with fine knurled focusing rings, engraved white and red distance and aperture numerals, and a small red dot on the front. Minimal, no clutter, brass showing through worn paint on edges. Lettering is a small script logo in white. Mood is understated, crafted and timeless.
In the style of Hasselblad, Swedish medium-format cameras. A cubic body with a square profile, finished in satin black paint with bright chrome trim or all-black crinkle texture, topped with a folding waist-level viewfinder hood or a pentaprism. A large detachable square film magazine fits the back and a big round lens with chrome or black barrel, coloured aperture and shutter rings, and engraved white numerals mounts at the front. Winding crank on the side, a single large shutter button, and a bold white capital wordmark. Special editions use bright colours or exposed carbon. Mood is serious, rugged, professional and sculptural.
In the style of Polaroid, instant film cameras of the 1970s and 1980s. Bulky, sleek folding or boxy bodies in beige, white or black plastic with a dark leatherette grip panel, a large single lens set above a bright rectangular flash bar and a big red rectangular shutter button. A chocolate-brown and tan palette is typical, with a rainbow stripe in red, orange, yellow, green and blue across the front. Film prints are square with a thick white border and a wide chin at the bottom, glossy surface, soft warm washed-out colours and slightly faded saturation. Lettering is bold rounded sans-serif in white. Mood is nostalgic, friendly and snapshot-casual.
In the style of Nikon, Japanese single-lens reflex cameras of the 1960s to 1980s. Rugged, boxy bodies with a pentaprism hump, black enamel or silver chrome finish, and a leatherette covering with fine pebble grain. Heavy metal knurled dials on top for shutter speed and film rewind, a chunky film advance lever, and a bold yellow and black stripe on the lens mount. Lenses are black barrels with deep rubber-ribbed focus rings and engraved white, orange and green numerals. Wordmark is bold white capital letters on the prism front. Mood is rugged, professional and mechanical.
In the style of Canon, 1970s and 1980s Japanese film cameras. Compact boxy SLR bodies with a black or silver top plate, wood-grain or fine black leatherette covering, rows of tiny metal dials and small silver buttons, and a red-lettered diagonal bar logo. Lens barrels are black with subtle knurling and a red ring accent around the front of professional lenses. Bodies have a clean rectangular layout with a large angular pentaprism, a round film-speed dial and a thick advance lever. Lettering is a stylised red script mark. Mood is dependable, practical and quietly handsome.
In the style of Fujifilm, retro-styled digital cameras. Compact rangefinder-style bodies with a flat top plate in silver and black, finished in matte black paint over metal with a fine textured grip. Many machined aluminium dials sit on the top plate, each with engraved numerals for shutter speed, exposure compensation and film simulation, and a ring of chunky aperture rings around the lens barrel. A small offset viewfinder in the corner, red ruler-like labels, and a neat pebbled leatherette in black. Lettering is crisp white capitals. Mood is tactile, classic, clean and understated.
In the style of Casio, Japanese digital watches and calculators. Lightweight resin cases in black, grey, silver or bright primary colours with squared-off octagonal or rectangular bezels, grey-green LCD displays showing chunky segmented digits, and small rubber push buttons on the sides labelled in tiny white capitals. Resin straps with moulded loops, steel bracelet versions with a gold finish, and tough rubberised protective bezels in G-shock style. Calculators have grid layouts of coloured keys in grey, orange and blue. Printed text is dense, small and technical, with many function labels around the dial. Mood is cheap, functional, geeky and indestructible.
In the style of Seiko, Japanese wristwatches of the 1960s to 1980s. Stainless steel cases with sharp faceted lugs, polished and brushed surfaces, and a clear domed crystal. Dials come in deep blue, black, silver, champagne or sunburst green with fine radial brushing, applied baton hour markers filled with luminous paint, a day and date window at three, and slim bright hands with a central sweep seconds. Divers have a rotating bezel with luminous pip and chunky bracelet links. Dial text is small silver capitals and a wordmark in stylised sans-serif. Mood is clean, practical, quietly sophisticated and slightly retro.
In the style of Rolex, Swiss luxury steel and gold watches. Round robust cases of polished and brushed stainless steel or yellow gold with a fluted or smooth bezel, a screw-down winding crown protected by shoulders and topped with a small crown logo, and a magnifying cyclops bubble over the date at three. Dials are glossy black, white, blue, green or champagne with applied polished hour markers and luminous dots, plus broad sword-shaped hands. Three-link Oyster-style bracelets with satin centre links and polished outer links. Lettering is crisp small serif capitals in gold or white. Mood is solid, imposing, polished and status-heavy.
In the style of Omega, Swiss sports and dress watches. Round steel cases with clean faceted lugs and a polished or brushed finish, domed hesalite-like crystals, and tachymeter bezels in black aluminium with white numerals. Dials are matte black or silver with contrasting sub-dials in chronograph models, baton hands, applied index markers with luminous infill, and a small Greek letter logo at twelve. Straps in black leather with white stitching or steel bracelets with tapering links. Lettering is crisp, slim white sans-serif. Mood is purposeful, elegant and exploratory with a vintage space-age touch.
In the style of Patek Philippe, Swiss haute horlogerie. Slim round or oval cases in polished rose gold, yellow gold or platinum with elegant fine lugs, a thin bezel and a refined hand-stitched alligator leather strap in brown or black. Dials are cream, silvered or deep blue with finely guilloched surfaces, slender dauphine hands, applied gold Breguet numerals or baton markers, and subtle small seconds or moon-phase windows with small scripts. Cases have clean proportions with discreet crowns and a Calatrava-style cross mark. Typography is fine serif capitals and delicate cursive. Mood is refined, understated, heirloom and timeless.
In the style of Nomos Glashutte, German minimalist watches. Slim round stainless steel cases with thin straight lugs, a very narrow bezel and a flat domed crystal over a pure white or silvery-white dial. The dial has slender blued steel hands, thin black Arabic numerals in a clean geometric sans-serif and a tiny subsidiary seconds dial at six, plus a thin minute track ring. Straps in plain brown or cordovan leather. Sapphire back reveals a decorated movement with stripes and blued screws. Mood is spare, precise, calm and modern.
In the style of Junghans, German modernist clocks and watches. Round or rectangular dials with extremely clean layouts, slim baton or simple stick hands, thin black line markers or short numerals in a geometric sans-serif, and a dome-shaped crystal. Case in matte steel or gold with minimal detail. Colours are white, black, grey with occasional warm orange or yellow second hands. Table clocks are small cylinders or squares of off-white enamel or wood with tiny hole speaker-style detail. Mood is pared-back, functional and Bauhaus-like in discipline.
In the style of Smeg, Italian retro kitchen appliances. Curvaceous, rounded rectangular refrigerators, kettles and toasters in glossy pastel enamel colours such as cream, pastel blue, pink, mint green and red, with polished chrome handles, trim and rounded corners. Large thick metal rounded door edges recall 1950s American cars, with a bold chrome oval logo on the front. Controls are chunky chrome knobs and rounded toggles. Surfaces are shiny and seamless with a soft sheen. Mood is cheerful, nostalgic and kitchen-boutique fashionable.
In the style of KitchenAid, American stand mixers. A heavy cast metal body with a smooth curved bulbous top, a tilting head that pivots on a rear hinge, and a tall flat-bottomed steel or glass bowl with a curved handle. Glossy enamel in empire red, onyx black, white, pistachio, pastel blue and cream, with a polished chrome speed lever on the side and a round attachment hub at the front. Beaters and dough hooks in brushed silver. Stainless script lettering on the front. Mood is sturdy, generous and homey with a vintage curve.
In the style of Dyson, British high-tech household machines. Transparent cyclone chambers in clear plastic reveal the inner mechanics, set against bright coloured plastic parts in purple, fuchsia, yellow, nickel grey and iron. Parts are tubular and ribbed, with exposed engineering, smooth curved plastic moulding, visible fixings and large chunky wheels. Bladeless fans are thin glossy rings on a tall tapering stand. Small colour-coded buttons and tiny LCD readouts. Lettering is small and bold. Mood is engineered, futuristic, glossy and rather industrial.
In the style of Bosch Professional, German power tools. Hard bodies in a distinctive deep blue plastic with black rubberised soft grips and bold red logo marks. Compact drills and saws have ergonomic curved handles, textured grips with diamond patterns, and bright silver metal chucks. Labels are crisp white capital sans-serif text and small red warning stickers. Battery packs are blue and black with a small LED level gauge. Cases are tough blue-grey moulded boxes with silver latches. Mood is robust, ergonomic, cool and professional.
In the style of Makita, Japanese power tools. Strong teal-blue-green plastic housings with black rubberised handles and black battery packs, a red stripe on the label, and tough silver or steel metalwork. Shapes are compact and rounded with chunky triggers, ribbed overmoulded grips, and neat rows of small ventilation slots. Text is bold white capital lettering and a boxed brand mark. LED work lights and bright yellow warning labels. Mood is solid, workmanlike and cool teal.
In the style of DeWalt, American contractor power tools. Bright yellow plastic housings with black rubberised grips and black accent bands, boxy chunky shapes with strong ribbing and protective rubber bumpers. Battery packs are chunky yellow and black blocks with a small fuel gauge. Text is bold black block capitals on yellow, with heavy outlined logo panels, and small silver steel chucks. Cases are rugged yellow and black stackable boxes with big latches. Mood is loud, rugged, jobsite-ready and high-visibility.
In the style of Milwaukee Tool, American heavy-duty power tools. Signature bright red plastic housings with black rubberised grips and black battery packs, bold stripes of red against black, tough angular shapes with big chunky triggers and reinforced rubber guards. Lettering is large white or black block capitals in a heavy italic sans-serif. Metal parts are raw silver steel, with red-and-black latch cases. Mood is aggressive, rugged, masculine and industrial.
In the style of Fender, American electric guitars and amplifiers of the 1950s and 1960s. Offset double-cutaway or single-cutaway slab bodies with sleek contours, sunburst finishes fading from yellow to red to black, or solid Surf Green, Daphne Blue, Fiesta Red and cream. White or tortoiseshell pickguards, chrome knobs and bridge plates, maple necks with black dot markers, and a bold curved script logo on the headstock. Amplifiers have tweed or black vinyl cabinets, silver grille cloth, chrome corner trim and a row of cream or black knobs. Mood is bright, twangy, sunny California.
In the style of Gibson, American electric and acoustic guitars. Carved arched-top bodies with a rich, glossy cherry red or golden sunburst finish, cream binding around the edges, and a flamed maple top. Bold mother-of-pearl block or trapezoid inlays on a dark rosewood fingerboard, gold-plated hardware, twin chrome-covered humbucking pickups, four gold barrel knobs, and a swept-back headstock with a pearl inlay logo and crown shape. Mood is rich, warm, heavy and luxurious.
In the style of Marshall Amplification, British guitar amplifiers. Large black vinyl-covered cabinets with a woven basket-weave cloth grille in tan or black, trimmed with gold or silver piping and a bold script logo in white on a gold or black plastic panel at the top. Control panel with a row of chunky black skirted knobs with white numerals, a pilot light in a red jewel, and a rocker switch. Stacks of tall rectangular speaker cabinets set in a wall on stage. Mood is loud, stacked, rock and roll and imposing.
In the style of Moog Music, analogue synthesizers. Solid wood cabinets in dark walnut or black with a slanted control panel in black or silver, packed with chunky round knobs with white pointers, patch cords and jacks, small rocker switches, and printed white labels in a clear sans-serif. A long keyboard with white and black keys and a pitch ribbon. Colour accents are red, orange and white lines grouping the sections. Mood is warm, vintage, scientific and handcrafted.
In the style of Roland, 1980s Japanese electronic instruments. Dark grey or black angular metal and plastic cases with wide control surfaces, rows of tiny coloured plastic buttons in orange, red, blue and cream, slim sliders and small red LED displays. Drum machines have rows of small rounded square pads in red, orange, yellow and white. Printed labels use fine white lettering with grouped panel lines. Synthesizers have long keyboards, wedge-like profiles and a bold stripe with the model name. Mood is techy, club-ready and boxy.
In the style of Yamaha Synthesizers, 1980s digital keyboards. Long slim cream-white or black plastic bodies with flat membrane buttons in grey and a small blue-green backlit LCD display screen, slim data-entry sliders, and a full-length velocity keyboard. Silver lettering and a minimal printed grid of function labels, rounded edge corners and a three-tuning-fork logo. Mood is sleek, digital and early-electronic with soft grey and teal tones.
In the style of Teenage Engineering, Swedish electronic music gear. Flat, thin, rectangular aluminium or white plastic slabs with exact grids of small coloured rubber buttons in orange, grey and white, tiny round knobs and bright pixel displays. Orange and neon accents, silkscreened white and black technical lettering in a small monospaced sans-serif, minimal diagrams and fine line graphics. Rounded sleeves and playful pastel-coloured carry cases. Mood is clean, geeky, graphic and playful.
In the style of Nothing, transparent tech gadgets. Clear see-through plastic and glass casings that reveal internal components, circuit boards, screws and coils. Palette is white, black and transparent with a signature bright red dot accent. Phone backs have glowing white LED light strips arranged in segments around the camera. Typography is dot-matrix lettering with round dots forming monochrome numerals, and icons are pixelated. Edges are flat and squared with soft corners. Mood is stripped-back, nerdy, futuristic and quirky.
In the style of Samsung, Korean consumer electronics. Thin glossy glass slabs with curved edges, metallic frames in black, silver, gold and blue, vivid AMOLED screens with saturated colours and nearly invisible bezels. Camera modules stand out in separate circular lenses on a vertical or horizontal strip. Televisions and appliances use thin brushed metal and glossy black or white panels with minimal touch controls. Interface graphics use rounded square icons, smooth sans-serif type and soft pastel colour gradients. Mood is polished, bright, flat-glass and modern.
In the style of Xiaomi, Chinese consumer electronics. Simple matte white plastic or glass shapes with softly rounded corners, orange squared-rounded logo block, and small flat circular lens bumps. Mood is clean, affordable-minimal and friendly.
In the style of Bose, American audio products. In the style of Cessna, light single-engine high-wing propeller aircraft with a boxy but friendly white fuselage, a strut-braced high wing, tricycle landing gear with streamlined fairings, a two-bladed spinner and propeller, a tall angular tail, large windows wrapping the cabin, painted racing stripes along the fuselage in red, blue, gold or green, small registration numbers on the fuselage, and a simple practical general aviation feel.
In the style of Sikorsky, rugged helicopters with a large rounded nose and bulbous cockpit glazing, a tall single main rotor with thick blades, a long tapering tail boom with a small tail rotor, stub sponsons housing wheels, olive drab or grey military paint, or white and red rescue paint with large black and white stencilled numbers, riveted aluminium skin, big exhaust and intake fairings, utilitarian heavy-lift character.
In the style of Concorde, a supersonic delta-wing airliner of the 1970s with a needle-like drooping nose that tilts down, a sleek white pure ogival delta wing, four underslung engines with sharp rectangular intakes, a tall slender white fuselage with tiny oval windows, a tapered tail fin with a thin blue line along the sides, pointed tail cone, and an elegant knife-slim, futuristic and almost bird-like profile.
In the style of Kenworth, long-nosed American conventional heavy trucks with a tall upright chrome grille, flat sloped hood with chrome edge strips, big rectangular chrome mirrors, a roomy sleeper cab, two tall stainless exhaust stacks behind the cab, deep two-tone paint with a bold stripe, polished aluminium wheels, round headlamps, a chrome bumper and visor, polished and proud highway giant.
In the style of Peterbilt, American long-nose heavy trucks with a distinctive long sculpted hood, a bold oval chrome grille with vertical louvres and rounded corners, a round chrome-edged headlamp, curved fenders, a tall chrome exhaust stack, a big chrome bumper, brilliant candy paint in black, red, blue or green with fine pinstripes and gold lines, a sleeper cab, polished tank covers, classic bold highway stance.
In the style of Scania, Swedish heavy lorries with a tall flat-fronted cab, a broad wide grille with a bold horizontal chrome bar, angular narrow headlamps, a rounded roof fairing with deflectors, a wide and tall windshield, a high-roof sleeper cab, a silver and blue griffin emblem on the grille, long lines of cab side skirts, tidy livery in solid colours such as white, red, blue and yellow, and crisp modern Nordic engineering.
In the style of Mack Trucks, American heavy trucks with a blunt bulldog nose, a hood that curves down with a chrome bulldog hood ornament standing on top, a bold upright chrome grille with horizontal bars, round chrome headlamps, short heavy fenders, thick chrome bumper, a stubby cab, bright paint in red, yellow, blue and black, stainless exhaust stacks and an unmistakable tough, muscular, no-nonsense stance.
In the style of John Deere, farm tractors and combines with a deep green body and bright yellow wheel rims and trim, a bold leaping deer logo on the hood, a boxy hood with a vertical grille, a rounded fuel cap, large rear tyres with deep lugs, a tall exhaust stack, an open platform or cab with glass panels, yellow stripes, and robust hardworking farm machine proportions with a clean agricultural palette.
In the style of Fendt, premium German farm tractors in a bright, glossy lime green with matte black accents, high-polish paint, rounded sleek bonnet with sharply cut headlamps in angular LED strips, a big glass cab with a curved panoramic windscreen, a metallic silver and black grille with a wide horizontal slot, clean modern bodywork with minimal clutter, big tyres and sharp precision engineering.
In the style of Massey Ferguson, mid-century tractors painted in bright red and grey, a rounded bonnet with a vertical slatted grille and a red body, grey wheels and chassis, grey or silver engine, chunky rubber tyres with deep tread, a simple open seat and steering wheel or small safety cab, an exposed exhaust pipe, bold block lettering on the bonnet side, and a plain sturdy post-war farming look.
In the style of the Routemaster double-decker bus, London buses in deep pillar-box red with a cream or pale band between the decks, a rounded boxy body with big curved corners, tall rounded front with a simple radiator-style grille, an open rear platform with a pole, large destination blind boxes above the windscreen, a fleet number and roundel fixed to the side, deep sliding windows, stencil-like route numbers, and a nostalgic London transport look.
In the style of Greyhound, American intercity coach buses with a streamlined stainless steel body with long fluted corrugated sides, a low rounded roof, big tinted side windows, a front destination sign above the windshield, a leaping dog silhouette emblem in the side panels, a blue and silver livery with white stripes, polished aluminium panels, rounded fenders, and an optimistic mid-century road travel look.
In the style of Great Western Railway, British steam locomotives with a gleaming Brunswick green paint scheme, polished brass dome and safety valve cover, a copper-capped chimney, black smokebox, red buffer beam with number plates, a curved copper-topped dome, orange and black lining on the cab and boiler, a tall brass-edged nameplate on the splasher, cream and chocolate brown carriages, and a Victorian-inspired proud engineering elegance.
In the style of Shinkansen, Japanese high-speed bullet trains with a long aerodynamic duck-bill nose, clean white bodies with a thin blue or colour stripe along the windows, a smooth continuous body with flush windows and doors, a streamlined cab with narrow wraparound windows, rounded cross-section, low skirts hiding the bogies, a polished near-seamless surface, and a futuristic yet calm precision look that evolved from the rounded bullet shape to long beak noses.
In the style of TGV, French high-speed trains of the 1980s with a pointed sloped nose and sleek aerodynamic power cars, articulated slim carriages, an orange and silver-grey livery with a bold orange belly and a dark grey roof, a long tapering sloped roof, a streamlined pantograph, a small oval windscreen set deep into the nose, flush windows and a futuristic wedge-shaped silhouette.
In the style of the Orient Express, luxury 1920s-30s sleeping and dining carriages with deep royal blue body panels and gold lettering and lining, a rounded barrel roof, polished brass handrails, glossy varnished teak and mahogany interiors with marquetry panels, brass luggage racks, velvet seating in burgundy, crisp white table linen with crystal and silver, art deco lamps with frosted glass tulip shades, and an opulent romantic travel atmosphere.
In the style of Riva, Italian wooden runabout motorboats of the 1960s with a long, slim, glossy mahogany hull, a varnished teak and mahogany deck in a bright caramel grain, a curved windscreen framed in polished chrome, white leather or cream and red upholstery, chrome cleats and fittings, pinstripe white or gold lines, a swept stern with a flag staff, a rounded tail deck over the engine bay, and a glamorous Riviera look.
In the style of Chris-Craft, American wooden runabouts with varnished mahogany hulls in a rich red-brown tone, polished planking with white cotton-caulked seams, a chrome-framed windscreen, chromed cleats, bow lights and flagpole, tufted leather seats in red and cream, a rounded transom with a script name plate, fine hull lines with a slim chine, twin cockpit decks, and a classic lakeside summer glamour.
In the style of Sunseeker, British luxury motor yachts with a sleek, low, sporty profile, a raked bow with an aggressive forward slope, long white fibreglass hull and decks with dark tinted wraparound windows, bold swooping stripe lines along the hull, polished stainless rails, a teak-clad aft deck, a flybridge with sunpads, lacquered interiors in cream leather and high-gloss wood, and a high-glamour marina look.
In the style of Boston Whaler, unsinkable fibreglass fishing and day boats with a wide stable two-hull catamaran-style shape, a white gelcoat hull with a thin navy or blue boot stripe, a high flared bow, a simple centre console with a stainless steel rail and leaning post, a small helm, grey non-slip decks, an outboard engine on a bracket at the stern, rod holders and cleats and an honest, tough, practical look.
In the style of NASA, American spaceflight hardware of the 1960s-70s, with a tall white rocket marked with black roll patterns and a red, white and blue flag, a golden foil-wrapped conical capsule, bright white spacesuits with orange or silver details, a red worm-shaped logotype or a blue circle with a swoosh and stars, riveted aluminium panels, dark gloss heat shield, and exposed cabling and engine nozzles set against black space.
In the style of SpaceX, modern reusable rockets with a gleaming smooth white cylinder painted in a bold clean finish, slender black interstage and landing legs, sooty carbon-streaked engine sections after reentry, a minimalist all-white or polished stainless steel body, a rounded nose cone, slim grid fins, a capsule with a tapered silhouette and black heatshield, minimal bold sans-serif lettering and a sleek futuristic aesthetic.
In the style of the Soviet space programme, 1960s rockets and spacecraft with a spherical capsule with portholes, a pea green and metallic silver bare hull, a cluster of four tapering boosters around a central core, a bold red hammer and sickle and CCCP lettering in red capitals, brushed orange-tinted foil, white painted rocket sections with stencilled numbers, cable bundles, rivets and utilitarian industrial heavy-engineering simplicity.
In the style of Pullman, American luxury railway sleeping cars of the early 1900s, with deep Pullman green paint, elaborate gold-leaf lettering and lines along the sides, a clerestory arched roof with small high windows, a rear observation platform with a railing, brass fittings, plush interiors in walnut with green or burgundy upholstery, gaslight-style lamps, and heavy steel clad rolling stock with a formal Gilded Age elegance.
In the style of Piaggio Ape, tiny Italian three-wheeled light trucks with a single front wheel and a rounded cab, scooter-style handlebars or a bench seat, a pressed-steel body in pastel mint, cream, light blue, or sun-faded yellow with a small tin cargo bed behind, rounded fenders, a tiny windscreen, chrome trim on the headlamp, and a quaint, cheerful Italian village feel.
In the style of Zeppelin, rigid airships of the 1920s-30s with an enormous silver cigar-shaped hull stretching on a lightweight frame covered in taut aluminium-doped cloth, a small streamlined gondola beneath the belly with rows of windows, four cruciform tail fins, propeller engine pods on struts, black bold Gothic lettering along the hull and a red flag on the tail, with a stately and monumental majestic presence.
In the style of Noritake, hard white porcelain tableware of the early twentieth century export period, glossy bone-white glaze with a faint bluish translucency, formal dinner sets with rounded lidded tureens, scalloped plates and footed cups with closed loop handles and small thumb curls. Decoration is hand painted and gilded, with wide borders of heavy gold, cobalt blue and deep rose red, central panels of lake scenes with cottages and trees in sunset orange and teal, and moriage work where slip trailed dots and beads of enamel stand proud of the surface like raised pearls. Later Art Deco sets turn angular, with stepped triangles, black and orange bands, stylised fans, sunbursts and parrots in pearly lustre glazes with a rainbow sheen. Lids carry bud or fruit finials. The underside shows a small green or magenta backstamp of a laurel ring around a letter M. Mood is refined, ornate, glittering and slightly nostalgic, like a treasured cabinet set from a grandmother house.
In the style of Shiseido, Taisho and early Showa period cosmetics packaging and print advertising, where Art Nouveau curves meet Art Deco geometry. Face powder tins and glass perfume bottles have faceted shoulders, domed stoppers and slim silhouettes, with gilt metal caps and lacquer red or ivory boxes tied with ribbon. Graphics use a stylised camellia flower mark in white or gold on red, thin elegant hand drawn lettering in an Art Nouveau block script, sinuous floral borders, and flat colour illustrations of slender women with swept up hair and long necks in kimono or Western dress, painted in muted rose, jade green, indigo and warm cream. A rainbow of seven powder shades lines up in matching round boxes. Surfaces are glossy, with gold foil edging and delicate line work, and photography is soft and monochrome with theatrical lighting. Mood is luxurious, modern for its time, feminine and carefully composed, with generous empty margins around small precise decorative motifs.
In the style of Zojirushi, Japanese kitchen appliances and vacuum flasks with a calm, trustworthy domestic look. Rice cookers are plump rounded boxes or domes with soft corners, in cream white, pearl grey or brushed stainless steel with a single dark glass or black panel carrying a small pale green backlit display and simple rounded buttons. Vacuum bottles are slim cylinders in satin stainless steel or soft pastel enamel with a flip top push button lid, a sturdy folding carry handle and a small shoulder strap. Hot water dispensers are tall tanks with a smooth glossy body and a neat spout lever. The company mark is a small side view elephant silhouette beside a red or black wordmark in friendly rounded letters. Surfaces are matte or satin, never flashy, with fine seams and tidy rubber gaskets. Instruction icons are tiny line pictograms. Mood is gentle, practical, clean and quietly cheerful, with the reassuring weight of well made kitchen equipment.
In the style of Tamiya, plastic scale model kits and radio controlled car boxes of the 1970s and 1980s. Box tops show a single vehicle or tank painted in rich illustrated realism on a plain white background, with crisp edges, glossy highlights, believable dirt, rust and exhaust staining, and dramatic low angles. A thin black frame and a small logo of two stars, one red and one blue, sit in the corner with clean sans serif lettering in black, red and blue. Inside, parts hang on pale grey, olive or black plastic sprues with sharp moulded rivets, hexagonal bolt heads, fine panel lines and neat decal sheets in white, red and yellow. Buggy and truck bodies are translucent polycarbonate shells painted from the inside in bright racing red, yellow and white with big sponsor-style stripes and number roundels, over chunky knobby tyres, silver aluminium dampers and exposed black plastic chassis. Instruction booklets use tidy exploded line drawings. Mood is precise, enthusiastic, hobbyist and sunny.
In the style of Kewpie, Japanese mayonnaise packaging built around a chubby baby doll mascot. The soft squeeze bottle is clear or translucent white plastic with a flexible body, a bright red screw cap and a star shaped nozzle opening that squirts fine ridged lines. Its label is mostly white with the red wordmark and a small round doll figure with a cheerful face, wide set eyes, rosy cheeks, plump rounded belly, tiny wings and a single curled tuft of hair on top, drawn in simple flat outline with pink and cream fill. Older glass jars have a tin lid and a paper label with the same doll in blue and red. Mesh bags of the doll shape show an orange red net lattice cross hatched pattern. Advertising uses pale yellow backgrounds, egg yolk golds and fresh vegetables arranged in neat circles. Mood is cute, wholesome, babyish and nostalgic, with soft round forms, a limited red, yellow and white palette and a clean modern food-photograph finish.
In the style of Yakult, tiny single serve probiotic drink bottles. The bottle is a squat pale plastic vessel about the size of a thumb joint, with a rounded shoulder, a ribbed and faceted waist like a small cut-glass tumbler, and a wide mouth sealed by a thin foil lid in white with red print. A glossy white wrap label carries the wordmark in bold red letters with a soft pink and blue accent band, a small drop or bubble motif and tiny fine print in black. The drink inside is pale milky peach. Bottles are sold in tight multipacks of five or ten bound in a printed paper sleeve, or arranged in rows on a shop fridge shelf, so the red and white pattern repeats. Delivery carts and carry boxes are white and pale pink with the same red lettering. Everything is clean, bright, hygienic and friendly, with rounded forms, plenty of white, one hot red accent and a mild medical-nutrition feeling.
In the style of Nissin Foods, instant noodle packaging of the 1970s and 1980s. Noodles come in a tall wide-mouthed disposable cup of foamed white polystyrene with a printed paper wrap in bold red, yellow and white, big italic chunky lettering in red outlined in white or black, and a bright photo or illustration of steaming noodles with shrimp, egg and green spring onion. A peel back foil lid in silver or printed paper is held down by a thin tab. Bags use shiny metallic film with stripes of red and yellow, thick sans serif titles slanting upward and a round or oval window showing the golden crimped noodle block. A square block of curly fried noodles sits on a plain table beside a plastic fork. Colours are saturated, appetising and slightly garish, using hot red, mustard yellow, black and white with starburst flashes saying new or tasty. Mood is cheap, fast, fun and nostalgic, evoking late night snacks and convenience-store shelves.
In the style of Tomica, small diecast toy cars about the length of a finger sold in colourful window boxes. Each car is a metal body in one glossy solid enamel colour, such as white, red, yellow, light blue or metallic silver, with a plastic clear windscreen, silver painted bumpers and lamps, tiny printed badges, and a hinged door or boot that really opens. Wheels are chrome five spoke hubs on small black plastic tyres, sometimes wide racing style rims. Cars are mostly everyday Japanese saloons, hatchbacks, minivans, fire engines, ambulances, taxis and buses in their real livery with painted stripes and lettering. The box is a cardboard sleeve with a picture window, in the classic black background with yellow flaps, later red and white, with an illustrated action scene of the car on a road, white sans serif model name, and a number tab. Foreign cars use blue and white boxes with a small flag. Mood is tidy, bright, practical, collectable and a little retro.
In the style of Namco, golden age arcade games and cabinets of the early 1980s. Characters are simple round shapes with big expressive eyes, drawn in a chunky low resolution pixel grid on a pure black background. A yellow circle with a wedge mouth chases glowing dots through a neon blue outlined maze of rounded corridors, pursued by pastel ghosts in red, pink, cyan and orange with wavy skirts and blinking white eyes. Fruits such as cherries and strawberries appear as bonus items in flat bright pixel art. Space shooters show symmetrical insect aliens in blue, purple and yellow diving in formation against a star field of single twinkling dots. Cabinets are upright wooden boxes with side art of cartoon characters, a slanted black marquee with a rounded glowing logo, a tilted CRT with scanlines, a joystick with a ball top and a few round buttons. Mood is cheerful, bouncy, bright and friendly, with limited saturated colours, hard pixel edges and bleeps in the imagination.
In the style of Korg, analogue synthesizers and rhythm machines of the 1970s and 1980s. Instruments are long, shallow wedge shaped keyboards with a slanted metal control panel, brushed or anodised charcoal and silver, covered in rows of small round knobs, chunky slider caps and tiny coloured lever switches in orange, red, blue and white. Labels are screen printed in clean white or orange sans serif capitals with thin line diagrams of waveforms, envelopes and signal routing, and a row of patch sockets sits at the upper left. Side cheeks are dark stained wood or black vinyl clad. Keys are cream white and matte black with a firm square profile. Small red LED digits and amber or green indicator lights glow on the panel. Drum machines are compact grey boxes with rows of tiny rectangular rubber pads and a small knob bank. Mood is functional, technical, compact and precise, with a slightly futuristic hi-fi flavour of the late 1970s and the cool grey of 1980s digital workstations.
In the style of Kubota, compact tractors and farm and construction machines in a single unmistakable colour scheme. The body panels and bonnet are bright safety orange, sprayed in a glossy even enamel, over dark grey or black chassis, wheel centres and engine covers. Tractors have a short rounded nose with a slatted front grille, rectangular work lights, slim chrome exhaust stack, a flat step platform and a high-backed black seat under a canopy or glass cab with big wraparound windows. Tyres are deep chevron treaded agricultural rubber. The logo is a white capital wordmark on an orange rectangle with a thin grey band along the bonnet side, plus small white model numbers and warning decals in yellow and black. Mini excavators and skid steers follow the same orange body with black rubber tracks or tyres, boxy booms and bright steel buckets. Mood is sturdy, dependable, tidy and slightly cheerful, like well maintained equipment sitting in a green rice field.
In the style of Asics, Japanese performance running and court shoes of the 1970s and 1980s. The upper is a mix of smooth suede and light nylon mesh panels, sleek and low, with a slightly flared heel tab and a gently curved toe cap. Two crossed diagonal stripes in a contrasting colour run along the side of the shoe, usually white on navy, or red, yellow and blue on pale grey, with a thin scalloped overlay near the laces. Midsoles are thick wedges of cream or white foam, sometimes with a visible gel window or a ribbed heel, over a crepe or waffle patterned gum rubber outsole. Colourways are earthy and sporty, such as cream with burgundy, navy with sky blue, or grey with orange and lime. A small chunky italic wordmark in white sits on the tongue and heel, with an olympic-era retro feel. Mood is athletic, light, vintage, understated and slightly nerdy-cool.
In the style of Calpis, Japanese milky soft drink concentrate packaging built on a polka dot pattern. The bottle or carton is covered in an even field of round white dots on a rich blue ground, standing for a starry Milky Way, with the later version inverting to blue dots on white. A bold simple wordmark sits in a rounded white or blue rectangle, usually in a soft rounded sans serif. Glass bottles are tall and slim with a narrow neck and a screw cap, wrapped in a thin paper label bearing the dots. Plastic bottles and small paper cartons use the same pattern on a larger scale, with a glossy finish. The drink is cloudy white and is served in a tall glass with ice, a straw, and perhaps a pale blue ribbon of colour behind it. Fruit flavour variants shift the accent colours to orange, peach and green while keeping the dots. Mood is clean, fresh, summery, childlike and retro, with a limited blue and white palette and gentle geometry.
In the style of Citizen Watch, Japanese quartz and solar watches with a practical, refined look. Cases are round or slightly cushion shaped in brushed and polished stainless steel or titanium, with a flat sapphire crystal and a slim bezel. Dials are clean and legible, in silver white, deep navy, black or champagne, with applied baton hour markers, sharp sword hands in silver with pale luminous fill, a small date window at three, and a fine printed minute track. Divers add a rotating bezel with alternating smooth and serrated sections, bold luminous dots, a screw-down crown and a rugged rubber or steel bracelet. Fine dress models use dials of lacquered black with raised star or moon motifs, mother-of-pearl, or paper-textured white. Bracelets have brushed centre links and polished edges with a hidden clasp. Small printed text lines the dial in tidy thin capitals. Mood is reliable, modern, understated, engineered and quietly elegant, with lit-from-above photography and cool metallic shine.
In the style of Taiwan Beer, bottled lager with a clear, restrained identity. The classic bottle is a standard brown glass longneck with a white and green paper label bearing a small crest like emblem, a bold sans serif wordmark and a golden ring of wheat. Premium versions switch to a gold and white label, and a Gold Medal version uses green glass with a white, red and green label. Draft versions appear as a solid green bottle with printed text and no paper. Cans carry a white background with blue stripes and green lettering for the classic, or glossy gold, blue and white for others. Imagery includes malted barley ears, a gleaming gold medal rosette, and clean foam topped glasses against a plain pale ground. Beer halls are built in a log cabin style with wooden beams, long benches and stir-fried seafood on round tables, under neon green signs. Mood is cheerful, social, hot-evening and straightforward, with strong green, gold and white colour blocking.
In the style of Kweichow Moutai, ceremonial Chinese baijiu bottles and gift packaging. The bottle is a squat cylindrical white porcelain flask with a short neck, glossy milk white glaze and a slight shoulder curve, topped with a red ribbon tied over a wax or plastic seal, and often a tiny hanging tag or flying fairy emblem. A bright vermilion label wraps the middle, edged in gold, printing the name in large black or gold brush style Chinese characters with a flowing line of small text beneath. Special editions are made in red glaze, blue and white, or glossy black. Gift boxes are lacquer red hard cartons or wood with lining of yellow silk and gold embossing, closed with a white cord and sometimes a golden bow, with a bouquet of two small cups beside the flask. Mood is formal, festive, prosperous and traditional, with an auspicious palette of red, gold and white against dark polished tables and banquet settings.
In the style of Flying Pigeon, heavy single speed roadster bicycles of the mid twentieth century in China. The frame is a black enameled steel diamond with a double top tube on the gents model, or a low step through loop frame on the ladies model. Large 28 inch wheels have chrome plated steel rims and thin spokes, with a full cover chain guard, a black rear rack carrying a leather pad, mudguards that end in a pale cream tip, and rod operated rim brakes. The saddle is black leather on big coil springs, the handlebars are wide upright sweeps with black grips and a small bell. White pinstripes and a gold lined transfer line the frame, and the head badge is a stylised dove perched over two initials. Tyres are thick and cream-walled or black. Bicycles stand in long dense rows beside tree lined streets, or carry stacked loads. Mood is utilitarian, durable, plain, communal and nostalgic, in a muted monochrome palette of black, chrome and cream.
In the style of White Rabbit Creamy Candy, small milky chews in a double wrapper. The candy is a plump cream coloured rectangle wrapped first in a thin edible rice paper film, then in a waxed paper twist printed in white with red, blue and black hand lettering in both Chinese characters and Latin script. A naturalistic ink drawing of a white rabbit stands or jumps in front of an old fashioned artist palette, drawn with fine line shading in the manner of 1950s commercial art. The outer pack is a flat sealed pouch or a bright cardboard box in red and white with a large rabbit face with wide forward-looking eyes, a cartoonish cheerful animal on a pale blue sky background. Display tins show vintage Art Deco borders. Colours are limited to white, red, blue and black, printed with a slightly matte texture. Mood is sweet, nostalgic, modest, childlike and old Shanghai, evoking sweet shops, school bags and festive gift trays.
In the style of Tong Ren Tang, a centuries old Beijing traditional medicine house. Shopfronts have a long dark wooden lattice facade with carved eaves, lacquered red pillars and a horizontal black board with large gold brush calligraphy characters, flanked by tall hanging vertical signs and red paper lanterns. Interiors show floor to ceiling rows of small dark wood drawers with brass pulls, each labelled in black ink on white tags, a high counter with a brass scale and abacus, and glass jars of dried roots, fungus and flowers. Products are sold in rectangular cartons or round tins in imperial yellow, vermilion red and gold, with embossed dragon or lotus borders, and individual pills are sealed in round wax shells in brown, red or yellow, stamped with a small seal mark. Labels use traditional characters in calligraphic strokes and red seal stamps. Mood is imperial, scholarly, aromatic, sombre and prosperous, like an old apothecary of lacquer, brass and paper.
In the style of Cathay Pacific, a Hong Kong airline identity in green and white. Aircraft have a clean white fuselage with a thin jade green cheatline running under the windows, a dark green tail fin and a calligraphic brushwing emblem, a single stroke like a swept bird wing curving upward in white or pale green against the dark ground. The earlier version used a lettuce green and white two-tone with a bare metal belly and a small flag on the tail. Titles appear in an upright sans serif wordmark in dark green, with Chinese characters in a small simple script beside it. Cabins are carefully quiet, with warm timber toned panels, bottle green economy seats with pale head covers, flat beds and soft cream leather in business, and thin gold accents. Crew uniforms are crisp, in dark green, with a neat scarf and a small gold wing pin. Mood is calm, urbane, polished, glassy and travelled, set against the harbour skyline and clouds at dusk.
In the style of Hong Kong Tramways, narrow double decker electric street trams that run along a dense city road. Each tram is tall and slim, with an upright boxy body, two storeys of tall rectangular windows in slim frames, a slightly curved roof, a short pole of trolley on top, and open balcony fronts on the upper deck. The lower body is painted deep forest green, with a cream or pale yellow stripe along the window line, and the upper front carries a square destination box with white characters on black. The most distinctive sight is the full body advertising wrap in saturated colours, glossy photographs and bold Chinese and English lettering that covers the entire flank, so each tram looks like a rolling poster. Interiors have wooden slatted seats, brass handrails, a plain bell cord and wooden floors, while the roof is open to the street with a brisk breeze. Mood is busy, nostalgic, neon-lit and crowded, with the tram sliding beside tower blocks.
In the style of Star Ferry, small wooden double deck harbour ferries on a crowded Asian harbour. Each vessel has a low, broad hull with a rounded overhanging bow, a white upper cabin and a narrow upper deck with a canvas or wooden awning, ringed by a thin white railing. The hull and trim are painted deep bottle green below a clean white superstructure, with a gold rubbing strake, black funnel top, brass fittings and cream life rings. Names in white Latin letters and Chinese characters appear on the bow, always with the word star as part of them. Decks have slatted wooden benches that can flip to face either way, with a small ticket booth and a rope barrier at the pier. Interiors show creamy paint, rows of varnished timber seats and round portholes. The piers are green steel sheds with clock towers. Mood is leisurely, nostalgic, salty and civic, with skyscrapers behind, hazy sky and water in dark teal.
In the style of Din Tai Fung, a Taiwanese dumpling restaurant with an exacting, clean presentation. Dumplings are small steamed buns with paper-thin translucent skins, each gathered by hand into a tidy spiral of eighteen fine pleats with a little knot at the top, sitting in a round bamboo steamer lined with a leaf or paper, with glints of broth showing. Open glass-walled kitchens show white-uniformed cooks in caps, masks and gloves rolling dough on stainless steel tables under bright white light, weighing each piece on small scales. Dining rooms are modern, with pale oak tables, blond wood screens, red brushed lettering signs, dark slate floors and slatted lanterns, and tea served in plain white cups. Condiments come in tiny dishes of black vinegar with sliced ginger. Menus are glossy cards with photographs. Mood is meticulous, hygienic, bustling and warm, with a limited palette of cream, bamboo tan, steam white and a single red accent.
In the style of Lee Kum Kee, Chinese sauce bottles and jars with a heritage label. The signature oyster sauce bottle is a clear glass or plastic bottle with a short neck, red screw cap, and a wide wraparound paper label in rich red with gold border lines. A circular central picture shows a woman and a boy on a small flat boat on a river, drawn in a simplified storybook engraving with fine black outline and flat gold, green and cream fills, around which sits the name in big golden brush strokes and Latin capitals. Other sauces keep the same red label, gold ribbon banner and small seal stamp for chilli, soy and hoisin. Gift tins are red square cans with gold lettering and traditional pattern borders. The sauce itself is glossy dark brown. Food shots show wok flames, green vegetables and shiny glazed meats on white plates. Mood is trusted, family-run, festive and old-fashioned, with an auspicious red and gold palette and dense decorative labels.
