In the style of Kenya Airways, an African airline identity in red, white and black. Aircraft have a white fuselage with the airline name in black italic capitals along the forward body, and a bright red tail fin carrying a stylised K inside a circle, in white and black. Older aircraft had four coloured stripes of red, green, black and white running the length of the fuselage in the colours of the national flag. Engines are red or white. A small flag appears near the cockpit, along with the slogan about pride of Africa. Cabins use red and charcoal seats with white head covers, grey carpets, and patterned fabrics with woven motifs. Crew wear red uniforms with black and white scarves and neat caps. Mood is warm, hospitable, proud, bright and savannah-like, with red against clear blue sky.
In the style of Castle Lager, a South African lager with a heraldic castle. The bottle is a brown glass longneck with a metal crown cap and a paper label with a cream, gold and green design, with a fortress of crenellated towers and a flag in an oval seal above the name in strong capitals. Cans are glossy green or silver with the castle device and a strip of gold. The sister light beer comes in a green bottle with a cold-indicator on the label that changes colour. Imagery features braai fires, rugby fields, jazz clubs, township street scenes and dusty plains, with beer being poured into a glass with a creamy head. Bottle crates are yellow plastic with the logo in black and red. Mood is sociable, relaxed, sunny, outdoorsy and communal, with amber, green, gold and cream.
In the style of Da Gama Textiles, South African shweshwe printed cotton. The fabric is a stiff starched cotton with a papery finish, ninety centimetres wide, printed with all-over repeating patterns that look like an intricate engraving. The classic colour is indigo blue with white discharged motifs, alongside chocolate brown, deep red, plum, teal and mustard. Motifs are small geometric diamonds, squares, circles, stripes, flowers, paisleys and tiny dotted trellis designs, finely detailed from the pinned engraving technique and very regular. The reverse is a flat solid colour and bears small printed brand marks of three cats. Garments include full A-line skirts, wrap dresses, aprons, headwraps and wedding outfits with panels sewn side by side, edged in braid and ribbon. Mood is traditional, cheerful, communal, crisp and deeply colourful. The cloth holds sharp creases when ironed, shows a faint white chalky bloom along folds, and is often seen in village yards, hanging on lines in bright sun.
In the style of Basotho Blankets, thick woolen blankets worn as wraps in Lesotho. The cloth is a heavy, tightly woven wool or wool blend with a soft nap, rectangular, with fringed ends and a plain selvage. Patterns are bold and geometric, such as wide stripes, large diamonds, squares, stylised leaf shapes, flowers and crowned shields. Classic designs include a cream and mustard ground with dark stripes, burgundy and navy blocks, a bold gold and black chevron, and a Victorian crown pattern in blue, red and cream. Colours are strong and earthy, with ochre, maroon, navy blue, forest green, black and cream. The blanket is pinned at the shoulder with a large safety pin and draped over riders on horseback wearing rubber boots and a conical straw hat. Mood is proud, blanket-wrapped, mountain-chilled and heritage-rich, set against rocky highlands in cool light.
In the style of Ardmore Ceramic Art, flamboyant hand sculpted South African ceramics. Pieces are thrown, modelled and painted by several artists, and are teapots, lamps, vases, candle holders, platters and egg cups. Forms are exuberant and playful, covered with sculpted animals such as leopards, zebras, giraffes, monkeys, antelope, snakes and birds that climb handles, perch on lids or curl around spouts. The surface is covered with a dense riot of dots, lines and leafy patterns, painted in bright glossy colours, including cobalt, emerald, orange, purple, turquoise, red and gold, with iridescent scales and shimmering lustres. Flowers and vines twist across the body in fine relief. Edges are outlined in dark, and every piece looks like a miniature jungle. Mood is witty, lush, whimsical, tropical and highly decorative, bursting with colour and detail.
In the style of Kazuri, Kenyan hand made ceramic beads and pottery. Beads are rolled and shaped by hand from local clay, then glazed in glossy colours and fired, so each one is slightly unique and irregular. Shapes are round balls, discs, short tubes, cones, ovals, chunky cylinders and tiny seeds, with smooth glass-like surfaces and a visible hole. Colours include cobalt blue, cherry red, mustard yellow, forest green, turquoise, terracotta orange, black, white and speckled earthy brown. Some are hand-painted with swirls, dots, stripes and zigzags in contrasting glaze. They are strung into chunky necklaces, multi-strand bracelets, long beaded ropes and earrings on cotton thread or leather cord, often mixing many colours. Pottery includes mugs and bowls with matching speckled glaze. Mood is joyful, earthy, handmade, colourful and sunny.
In the style of EgyptAir, a national carrier identity of ancient mythology. Aircraft have a white fuselage, and a deep blue and gold tail fin carrying a golden falcon god, stylised in a flowing, wing-spread pose with a red head, blue feathers and a gold backdrop. The airline name appears in both Latin and Arabic script in dark blue on the forward body. The older livery had a warm band of red, gold and orange swirling around the window line and engines, giving a sunset glow. Cabins use blue and gold seats with Egyptian geometric motifs, pale wood trims, and gold emblem on the head covers. Crew wear smart navy uniforms with a hat or scarf in pharaonic gold. Mood is stately, ancient, desert-sunny and glowing, with lapis blue, gold and red.
In the style of Ethiopian Airlines, an African flag carrier identity. Aircraft have a pale grey and white fuselage, with a lower belly painted pale grey and a flowing band of green, yellow and red lines sweeping up toward the tail. The tail fin is deep green with a yellow and red stylised bird or wing emblem. The airline name is shown in Amharic script and Latin capitals in dark green on the forward body. Cabins feature green seats with woven patterned cloth in red and gold, white head covers and wooden panels. Crew uniforms are green, yellow and red with traditional white woven cotton shawls trimmed with coloured borders. Mood is highland-bright, welcoming, proud and colourful, with green, gold and red against clear blue sky. Boarding gates and posters repeat the three colour bands as flowing ribbons, and cargo aircraft carry the same bands on a simpler white body.
In the style of Royal Air Maroc, a Moroccan airline identity of red and green. Aircraft have a white fuselage with a vivid red tail fin carrying a green five pointed star inside a round white emblem, and a slim green and red stripe along the windows. The airline name appears in Latin and Arabic script, in bold dark red letters on the forward body. Cabins use deep red and green fabrics, geometric tile-inspired patterns in white and blue, and brass accents. Crew uniforms are smart dark red with a fine scarf and a decorative pin. Marketing images show blue skies over terracotta walls, tilework in blue, green and white, and palm trees. Mood is warm, hospitable, ornate and sunny, with red, green and white geometry. Brochures and lounge screens repeat the eight pointed star in carved wooden latticework, and ground vehicles at the airport carry a plain red band with the same green star.
In the style of Tusker Lager, a Kenyan lager with an elephant emblem. The bottle is a clear or brown glass longneck with a metal crown cap, carrying a paper label in cream and red with an upright elephant head in profile with long curved tusks, drawn in bold black ink, above the name in strong capitals. Cans and crates use red, gold and black with the same elephant, and glossy bottle neck wraps in gold foil. Bars and sign painters decorate walls with big hand painted logos and slogans in bold yellow and red. Photos show friends around a wooden table beneath thatch, with grilled meat and ugali, on dusty roadsides. Mood is communal, sunny, savannah, relaxed and proud, using red, gold, black and cream. Promotional crates are stacked beside kiosks with corrugated iron roofs, and coasters, bottle openers and branded plastic stools repeat the elephant in bold black on bright red.
In the style of Sapporo Brewery, Japanese lager packaging with a star emblem. Cans are tall and slim, with a black Black Label version in matte black with a bold gold star and name, and a standard silver can with a red star in a circle. Labels on brown bottles are cream and gold with a five pointed star and thin ruled borders. Special editions use a deep green with gold edging. Large format bottles come in silver foil wraps. The star is drawn in a crisp geometric outline, often placed at the centre of the can or bottle, with a wordmark in strong serif or slab capitals. Beer is golden with a thick white foam head. Photographs show frosted mugs on a counter in an izakaya, with yakitori skewers and paper lanterns. Mood is crisp, masculine, social, evening-lit and no-nonsense, with black, gold, red and silver.
In the style of Los Castillo, mid-century Mexican modernist silver and silverplate from a Taxco workshop. Heavy hand-hammered hollowware such as pitchers, creamers, sugar bowls, trays and lidded bowls, with sculptural animal forms like long-necked birds, seahorses, pelicans and fish whose bodies swell into smooth rounded volumes. Surfaces are bright polished metal with a warm slightly rippled hammered texture, set off by chunky cabochon inlays of deep green banded malachite, blue lapis and azurite pressed flush into the metal. Jewellery uses chunky link bracelets, cuffs and brooches in sterling silver married with copper and brass in interlocking geometric panels, stepped-fret and feathered-serpent motifs abstracted into bold simple shapes with strong negative space. Handles and feet are cast as stylised beaks, tails and fins. The mood is festive, organic and luxurious, like a 1960s cocktail party table, with a slightly imperfect handmade finish and visible tool marks rather than machined perfection.
In the style of Dona Rosa black pottery from San Bartolo Coyotepec in Oaxaca, hand-built barro negro vessels with a deep, mirror-like metallic black sheen. Clay is shaped by hand on a turning plate with no wheel, so forms are softly rounded and slightly asymmetrical, such as tall jars, round-bellied pots, vases with narrow necks, lidded urns, candle holders, whistles and the famous monkey-shaped mezcal flask. The surface is rubbed with a smooth quartz stone before firing in a smoky pit kiln, giving a silky gunmetal-to-jet shine with soft grey matte patches where the burnish is lighter. Decoration is restrained, with applied clay flowers, rosettes, twisted ropes and small handles, delicate pierced or cut-out lacework openwork on lamps, and fine incised lines. Unpainted pieces stay monochrome black or silver-grey, while some are coloured in flat bright paints. The look is earthy, sculptural and quietly elegant, an ancient shape language with a polished modern gleam, photographed against plain pale backgrounds.
In the style of the Angeles family workshop of San Martin Tilcajete in Oaxaca, carved and painted copal-wood alebrije animals. Light soft wood is hand-carved with machete and knife into lively mythical creatures such as jaguars, armadillos, owls, dogs, bears, dragons and winged beasts, often with a human face or braided hair on an animal body, caught mid-action while flying, scratching or leaping. Sizes range from palm-sized to over a metre. After carving, a smooth base coat is applied, then the whole body is covered in intricate fine-line ornament in vivid natural-dye and acrylic colours, magenta, turquoise, saffron yellow, orange, leaf green and black, in dense repeating patterns of dots, scrolls, flowers, plants, greek-key-like steps and geometric Zapotec motifs from ancient temple walls. Eyes are large and glossy, tongues and claws picked out in contrasting hues. The finish is a light satin, with every surface patterned and nothing left blank, giving a joyful, hallucinatory, highly saturated folk-art look.
In the style of Tenango embroidery from the Otomi communities of Hidalgo in Mexico, bold fantastical animal and plant scenes stitched on cloth. The base is usually plain white or cream cotton manta, sometimes black, red or blue, and the whole field is filled edge to edge with flat, outlined figures. Motifs are large stylised birds, deer, rabbits, dogs, horses, roosters, lizards, butterflies and fish, mixed with lush curling flowers, leaves, trees and tiny dots, arranged symmetrically or in mirrored pairs around a central tree. Colours are saturated and not realistic, with sunny yellow, grass green, cobalt blue, hot pink, orange, red and purple, often a single hue per figure or striped bands within one animal. The stitching is dense, tight satin and chain stitch with thick coloured thread, giving a slightly raised, filled-in look with bold dark outlines, as seen on cushion covers, table runners, bedspreads and wall hangings. The mood is folk, cheerful, playful and slightly naive, derived from ancient cave and bark-paper cut-out figures.
In the style of Wixarika Huichol beadwork and yarn paintings from western Mexico, ceremonial imagery rendered in dense blazing colour. Small glass seed beads are pressed one by one into a layer of beeswax and pine resin that covers carved wooden forms such as skulls, jaguar heads, deer, eagles, snakes, masks, bowls and gourds, leaving a shining mosaic surface with a slightly bumpy glittering texture. Flat yarn paintings use thick coloured wool pressed into a wax board in tight spiralling lines that follow each shape like contour maps. Motifs are deer, maize, peyote buttons, scorpions, five-petalled flowers, sun faces, rain clouds and snake bodies with diamond backs, with zigzag lines radiating from animals. The palette is neon-bright and contrasting, fuchsia, turquoise, lime green, electric blue, orange, yellow, red and black, with white outlines, filled completely so no background shows. The overall effect is psychedelic, symmetrical, intricate and intensely handmade, like a jewelled devotional object.
In the style of Granado, a Brazilian apothecary and perfumery with a nineteenth-century pharmacy look. Soaps, talcs, colognes and creams come in simple old-fashioned packaging, glass bottles with cork or screw stoppers, frosted apothecary jars, tin boxes and paper-wrapped bars. Labels are cream, white, pale pink or soft green paper with a thin double-line border, centred oval cartouches, fine ornamental scrollwork and curling flourishes, with text in elegant serif and decorative Victorian display lettering in deep burgundy, bottle green, navy or gold. Soap wrappers are printed with tiny repeating floral and botanical prints and sometimes a small crest with a crown. Products are displayed in shop interiors of dark polished wood cabinets, glass-fronted shelves, marble counters and tiled floors in black and white or terracotta. The mood is nostalgic, refined and slightly aristocratic, a clean tropical pharmacy from another century with muted pastel and old-gold accents and restrained, tidy typography.
In the style of Varig, the Brazilian airline livery of the jet age. The aircraft has a polished bare-metal lower fuselage with a white upper body separated by a thin navy-blue cheatline running along the windows, with the company name in large dark blue capital letters in a clean wide sans-serif above the windows. The tail is a vivid royal or cobalt blue field carrying a large circular compass rose emblem in white and gold with a star-like design, dark rays around a central disc, becoming a gold-yellow rose on a deep blue belly, engines and tail in the later 1990s version. A small green, yellow and blue national flag sits near the rear door, and a short word for the country appears in a thin italic script beside it. Earlier propeller planes show a winged mythical figure in a black circle on the tail and a bold black stripe on the nose. The look is clean, optimistic, glamorous and quintessentially 1960s intercontinental travel, with lots of reflective silver metal.
In the style of Wayuu mochila bags woven by cooperatives of women in La Guajira in Colombia. The bag is a soft round-bottomed shoulder bag shaped like a cylinder or bucket, crocheted in a tight single-strand spiral so the fabric is dense and slightly stiff, with a flat circular base and a flat wide strap in woven or braided cotton. A top band and strap are in one solid colour, while the body is covered in large bold geometric designs. Patterns are repeating diamonds, zigzags, stepped squares, crosses, labyrinth-like meanders, stripes and symmetrical motifs named for animals and desert elements, stitched in thick yarn. The palette is joyful and high-contrast, hot pink, turquoise, lemon yellow, orange, red, royal blue and lime green against black, white or cream, often with a bold gradient or striped panel. Finishing details are a braided drawstring with large tassels, or a twisted rope handle with pompoms. The look is handmade, tropical, energetic and slightly raw, with visibly wavy lines and woollen texture.
In the style of the Andean weaving cooperative of Cusco in Peru, hand-woven backstrap-loom textiles in natural dyed wool. Pieces are long narrow bands, square blankets, shoulder bags, belts, ponchos and table runners made of sheep, alpaca or llama yarn with a slightly fuzzy hand-spun texture. The warp-faced weave shows crisp horizontal stripes of plain colour alternating with bands of dense pattern, patterned panels of diamonds, zigzag lines, small stepped figures, llamas, birds, lakes drawn as flowers inside diamonds, Milky Way meanders and tiny geometric motifs which are reversible on both sides. Colours are earthy plant-dye shades, deep indigo and sky blues, cochineal reds, pinks and purples, yellow-greens, mustard, walnut brown, cream and charcoal, and each community has its own dominant palette such as maroon with white or red with green. Edges are finished with braided cords and tassels. The look is rich, rustic, rhythmic and symmetrical, a precise ancient textile language rendered in warm natural tones.
In the style of Chulucanas pottery from northern Peru, a pre-Columbian revival ceramic with smoky resist decoration. Pieces are smooth rounded vessels, bottle-necked jars, plump vases, owls, birds, fish, human figures and bowls, thin-walled with softly bulging bodies. The surface is polished to a glossy sheen by rubbing before firing. Decoration comes from a negative resist technique where a liquid clay slip is painted on areas that stay light, then mango-leaf smoke blackens the bare areas in a second firing, giving crisp contrasts of cream, orange-tan, chocolate brown and glossy black. Typical patterns are polka-dot spots, tight rows of dots, checkerboards, stripes, spirals, wavy lines, stepped angles and little fish or leaf motifs, often in two colours only, black on tan or tan on black. Some pieces are smoked solid black or painted in several earth colours. The look is minimalist, tactile, graphic and folk-modern, like a contemporary sculpture from an ancient workshop.
In the style of the Cuban cigar house Cohiba, a severe, luxurious tobacco-product look. The cigar band is a narrow strip with a black background and a bold yellow-gold geometric lettering that spells the name in angular, jagged capitals inspired by an indigenous Taino face mask, with a stylised yellow-and-black chequered pattern inside the main letters. The band wraps around the cigar in a diamond or tapered shape beside a smaller white and gold secondary band. The box is flat black or dark brown with rich tobacco-leaf lacquer, clean edges, brass-like gold foil embossing, a matte black label and a hologram sticker. The tobacco itself is smooth, shiny, dark oily brown wrapper with fine veins. A secondary dark green and white warning strip crosses the lid. Interiors show rows of cedar-wood cigars wrapped in thin cedar sheets, a deep red and brown glow, and a quiet, exclusive, masculine mood with a restrained black, yellow and cedar palette.
In the style of Havana Club, Cuban rum packaging with an old-Havana, street-art flavour. The bottle is a tall squared-shoulder clear glass bottle with a sturdy cork and a small decorative neck tag, filled with amber or golden rum. The front label is cream or ivory paper with an aged, slightly weathered look, a big ornate nineteenth-century cigar-box-style cartouche with a gold and red border, a small oval vignette of a city fortress and a colonial tower, and lettering in tall wood-type capitals and rounded script. Contemporary versions add colourful screen-printed illustration, saturated flat inks in red, blue, yellow and green, hand-drawn musicians, dancers, palm trees, vintage cars and city skyline. Silver and gold rum labels use metallic foil and ribbed stamping. The overall mood is warm, rhythmic, nostalgic and tropical with a faded colonial architecture palette of mint green, terracotta, sun yellow, turquoise and sepia.
In the style of the Cuban film institute poster studio, hand-silkscreened posters from the 1960s and 1970s. Each poster is printed with a few flat, saturated colours in layers, with no gradients, bright red, yellow, cobalt blue, orange, green, pink and black, laid down one screen at a time on cheap rough paper with slightly uneven edges and visible misregistration. Imagery is bold, simplified and symbolic, such as a giant eye, a cut-out head, a hand holding a camera, a skeleton, a face split into colour planes, a single iconic object enlarged to fill the sheet, often with playful surreal collage-like juxtapositions and sharp graphic cut-out edges. Lettering is hand-drawn, chunky, irregular, sometimes stencil-like or distorted to match the theme, and sits in blocks of solid colour. The look blends pop art, psychedelia, Constructivist geometry and Latin satire, vibrant, witty, politically charged and strongly handmade.
In the style of Havanna, an Argentine alfajor and coffee house brand. The alfajor is a round sandwich of two soft pale cookies with a thick dark layer of dulce de leche in the middle, glazed fully in glossy dark chocolate or white meringue-like icing, sold individually wrapped in a shiny foil. The wrapper and tin boxes are a deep turquoise-blue or royal blue and white scheme with a warm cream, a large rounded white script logo in the form of a flowing signature over a navy background and slim gold or silver details, and the circle of a small orange rising-sun mark. The packaging of boxes has soft rounded rectangular shapes, with maritime references such as a seaside town and small gulls. The cafes are warm wood-and-brass spaces with dark wood counters, tall windows, beige marble tables and lamps with amber light. The mood is friendly, sweet and nostalgic seaside Argentina with a trustworthy clean cream and blue palette.
In the style of the Pomaire potters of central Chile, rustic hand-thrown terracotta cookware and figures. Pieces are heavy, thick-walled and unglazed or lightly burnished, in a warm reddish-brown to dark grey-brown clay with a smooth matte surface that darkens with use. Shapes are chunky and round, with pots with two ear handles, wide shallow bowls for stews, lidded casseroles, jugs, pitchers, plates, candle holders and flower pots. The best-known figure is a plump little three-legged pig money box with a slot on its back, alongside owls, hens, bulls, mermaids and small nativity figures made by pinching and moulding. Some are painted with simple flat colours or white slip dots and stripes, others left plain with finger marks and uneven rims. Kiln smoke gives mottled black clouds and variations. The look is earthy, handmade, humble and warm, and typically seen stacked on rough wooden shelves in an adobe market.
In the style of Guna women of the San Blas islands of Panama, reverse-appliqued mola panels. Each mola is a rectangular blouse panel made of two to seven layers of thin cotton cloth in contrasting solid colours, the top layer cut away in curving shapes so the layers beneath peek through, with edges turned under and hemmed with tiny nearly invisible stitches. The design fills the whole panel with an energetic horror vacui of tightly packed shapes in parallel concentric outlines, like a thick-lined labyrinth, depicting birds, fish, sea turtles, parrots, flowers, masks, abstract geometry and, in modern ones, cartoon characters or everyday objects. Colours are bright and fully saturated and placed in bold contrast, red against orange, black against yellow, green against hot pink, blue against white. Extra details include small slits, dots, zigzag borders, lattice patterns and embroidered eyes. The look is graphic, rhythmic, vibrant and strongly handmade, flat and symmetrical like stained glass.
In the style of Bacardi, a Cuban-born rum house with a heraldic bat emblem. The central symbol is a black bat with outspread wings seen from the front, drawn in a bold, simple, slightly gothic silhouette with a small head, set inside a red, white and black label. The label on a clear square-shouldered or tapered glass bottle is bright, clean white or cream with a red, black or gold border and big black block lettering, a small crest at the top and a tiny red wax-like seal around the cap. Premium rums use deeper amber liquid, gold-foil edging and aged cream labels with ornate serif lettering. Barrels are dark oak with iron hoops, and old advertising shows sunny beaches, palm fronds, cocktails in coupe glasses and bright red umbrellas. The Art Deco headquarters building is a tall tiled tower clad in blue-and-white geometric patterned tiles with a stepped crown. The mood is festive, tropical, confident and slightly retro.
In the style of the metalworkers of Croix-des-Bouquets in Haiti, flat decorative sculptures cut from recycled oil drums. Panels are made by flattening used steel drums, drawing a design with chalk and then chiselling and punching it with hammer and nail so the line work appears as a pattern of cut-through openings and raised ridges. Subjects are the tree of life, mermaids, birds, fish, sun faces, spirits with elaborate headdresses and crosses, drawn with symmetrical swirling leaves, scales, dotted outlines, hearts and curling vines. The metal is left in dark grey and rust-brown, sometimes polished to a rough silver with burn marks, or hand-painted in bright enamel colours like red, yellow and turquoise. Pieces are wall hangings in oval, round or irregular shapes with ragged cut edges, sometimes bent into three-dimensional animals and candle holders. The look is raw, intricate, spiritual and richly patterned, with a rugged industrial material turned into delicate lace.
In the style of Acoma Pueblo potters of New Mexico, extremely thin-walled hand-coiled ceramics with fine-line painting. Vessels are rounded jars with high shoulders and small mouths, bowls, canteens, seed pots and low wide shapes, with a very fine white slip surface that is smooth, matte and chalk-like. The walls are paper thin, and a finished piece rings clearly when tapped. Decoration is painted with a yucca-leaf brush in black and rusty orange or red-brown on the creamy white ground, in dense precise geometric bands. Motifs are fine-line lattices and hatching, interlocking triangles, parrots, thunderbirds, rainbows, stepped clouds, deer with a heart line and scrolls, with pattern bands wrapping horizontally around the shoulder and neck and leaving tidy empty zones at the base. Unglazed, low-fired and a little powdery, with slightly brown tonal variations from the fire. The look is crisp, disciplined, optical and graceful, like delicate op-art on earthenware.
In the style of San Ildefonso Pueblo black-on-black pottery from New Mexico. Hand-coiled, perfectly symmetrical jars, bowls and plates with a rounded, full body, a marked shoulder angle, a short narrow neck and a slightly flattened rim. The whole surface is burnished to a glass-smooth gloss and fired in smothering dung smoke to a deep jet black. Decoration is made by painting designs in a clay slip which stays matte after polishing, so the pattern shows as flat dull black against a shining black field, visible only as light shifts across it. The designs are a wide band around the shoulder with a coiled avanyu water serpent with horns, feathers, rain clouds, kiva steps, lightning zigzags, mountains and bird tracks. Some pieces are carved or etched through the surface, others are red or sienna with matte-and-polish contrasts. The look is sleek, dark, formal and sculptural, timeless, with subtle reflective effect and perfect balance.
In the style of the Toadlena Two Grey Hills Navajo weavings of New Mexico, a restrained natural-wool tapestry look. Rugs are flat-woven by hand on a vertical loom, very tightly and finely, in a rectangular format with a dense, almost smooth surface. The colours come only from undyed sheep wool carded and blended, so the palette is creamy white, light and dark grey, tan, camel, chocolate brown and jet black, with no bright dye. The design is a symmetrical central geometric field with diamonds, stepped zigzags, concentric rectangles and terraced crosses, framed by a plain border and a crisp inner outline, with small tapering corner details and often a single dark stripe on the edge. The pattern is balanced, precise and optical, with crisp straight edges and no fringe tassels at the ends. The look is calm, earthy, refined and architectural, like a desert landscape abstracted into straight lines.
In the style of the Cape Dorset Inuit printmaking and carving cooperative in Arctic Canada, bold stonecut prints and soapstone sculpture. Prints show simple powerful animals such as owls, geese, walruses, bears, seals, caribou, ravens and spirit figures with big expressive eyes and strong feathers drawn as repeating curved shapes, printed with flat ink in a limited palette on thin pale cream or white handmade paper. The ink is deep black, with blue-grey, ochre, red-orange, green and teal appearing as flat blocks, with a soft grain from the stone and a subtle ridge around each shape, often with generous empty paper space around them. Stone carvings are smooth, rounded, polished dark green-grey or black soapstone and serpentine in heavy, compact forms of bears, birds, hunters, mother and child, dancing figures and hybrid creatures. The mood is quiet, northern, graphic and dignified, with a simple, bold and direct line.
In the style of Cowichan Coast Salish hand-knitted sweaters from Vancouver Island in Canada. The garment is a thick, bulky, hip-length cardigan or pullover in chunky hand-spun undyed sheep wool, with a shawl collar, full front opening with large buttons or a zipper, and rounded set-in sleeves. The wool is slightly oily, with a rustic hairy texture and a sturdy feel. The colour palette is natural cream, charcoal, grey, brown and black, sometimes with a small amount of red or a bright accent. The central design is a broad yoke or back panel in intarsia-type knitting depicting a large bird such as an eagle or thunderbird, a deer, a whale, a wolf, a bear head or a geometric band of triangles and diamonds, repeated in horizontal stripes across the chest and around the cuffs. Edges have a ribbed band in a plain colour. The look is cosy, outdoorsy, woodsy and cabin-like, with the heavy weight of waterproof wool.
In the style of Reyn Spooner, reverse-print Hawaiian shirts. The shirt is a short-sleeved button-up with a pullover or full-front placket, a soft flat collar, left chest pocket and a straight hem, in a thick cotton-poly blend woven in a plain oxford-like texture. The print is applied to the inside of the cloth, so the outer face looks washed, faded and muted as if sun-bleached, with soft tonal colours, navy, forest green, dusty rust, burgundy, sand, teal and faded sky blue. Motifs are large-scale tropical leaves, palm fronds, monstera, hibiscus, ferns, bamboo, canoes, surfboards, hula scenes and island maps arranged in repeating patterns, often in a single colour family with a lighter shade for outlines. Buttons are coconut-shell or dark plastic. The mood is relaxed, understated, preppy and island-casual, a subtle quiet version of the aloha shirt rather than a loud one.
In the style of Kahala, a Hawaiian aloha shirt house. The shirts are short-sleeved with an open camp collar, straight hem, coconut-shell buttons and a chest pocket in which the pattern continues without a break, made from rayon, silk-like cotton or soft poplin. The prints are large and detailed, in vintage-poster fashion, with hibiscus, plumeria, bird of paradise, palm trees, monstera, bamboo, tiki masks, outrigger canoes, volcano views, sunset beaches, hula dancers, tapa-like geometric borders and quilt patterns. Colours are rich and warm, including turquoise, coral, teal, deep orange, mustard, palm green, navy and cream, often with a shaded or watercolour quality and a dark background for tropical flowers. Some designs reproduce mid-century lei-and-surf scenes in two or three tones. The overall look is relaxed, sunny and nostalgic, Waikiki in the 1950s, bright but warm, with a hand-drawn illustration feel.
In the style of Papunya Tula Artists of Australia, acrylic dot paintings of the Western Desert. The canvas is covered by dense fields of small round dots of paint applied with sticks, creating a shimmering, optical texture. Compositions are mapped from above, as sacred sites, with concentric circles, U-shapes for seated people, curving lines for travel paths, meandering parallel lines of water, tracks of animals and spreading wavy bands. The palette is earthy ochre, with red-brown, burnt orange, yellow, ochre, charcoal, black, white and cream, and later more vivid pink and blue. The background is often dark, and dots are dense and layered to hide secret meaning, with the picture reading either as a map or as an abstract field. The painted surface is matte, with slightly raised dots. The look is hypnotic, symmetrical, rhythmic, very graphic and very earthy, resembling aerial views of red desert country.
In the style of Penfolds, an Australian wine label with a classic traditional look. The bottle is a dark green Bordeaux style with sloping shoulders, topped by a tall foil capsule in deep red or black and a long tight cork. The label is a cream or off-white rectangle, with an elaborate oval or shield-shaped heading in thin black lines, an engraved-looking vignette, a signature-like stylised script, and neat serif capital lettering in black and dark red, with a vintage year and a bin number in bold numerals. Premium bottles have a heavy dark brown or deep burgundy label with a gold foil rule border, and the name is set in embossed gold capitals. Black wax or red wax seals appear at the top. The cellar scenes feature rows of oak barrels, dark brick vaults, copper pipes and bottle racks covered in dust, in a restrained palette of cream, burgundy, black, gold and green. The mood is established, serious and heritage-rich.
In the style of Arnotts, Australian biscuit packaging. The brand mark is a multi-coloured parrot perched on a T-shaped stand eating a biscuit, drawn in a late nineteenth-century engraving style with a red, yellow, green and blue plumage, inside a red oval or circle. Boxes and wrappers are bright and cheerful, a red base for chocolate biscuits, yellow for sweet ones and blue or green for crackers, with a bold white block rounded sans-serif title, large appetising photos of the biscuits and a swirl or ribbon behind. Biscuits themselves are distinctive shapes, a rectangular two-layer chocolate-coated finger with a pattern of fine diagonal ridges, round crackers with small holes in a neat grid, square plain crackers, teddy-bear shapes, round cream sandwiches and a large round orange-sectioned disc with sugar frosting. Tins are tall rectangular metallic boxes with printed scenes. The mood is homely, nostalgic and cheerful, a family lunchbox feel.
In the style of Billabong, Australian surf culture clothing. The core logo is a rounded script with a flowing wavy line running beneath, or a stack of the name in a slightly bubbly bold type, printed on boardshorts, tees and caps, often with a stylised wave swoosh in teal, blue or black. Boardshorts are long knee-length shorts with laced fly, a velcro-and-snap waistband, triple stitching and a small leg patch, made in quick-drying nylon or poly in bright colours and tropical flower, stripe or tie-dye prints. Colours are ocean tones, teal, navy, aqua, coral, lime and yellow against white, with sun-faded greys. T-shirts carry large screen-printed graphics of waves, palm trees, retro surf scenes, hand-drawn lettering, sunsets and cartoon creatures. Wetsuits are slim black neoprene with contrasting colour panels and sleek paneling. The mood is sunny, sporty, laid-back, youthful and sandy.
In the style of Bonds, Australian basic underwear and kids clothing. The core product is a plain cotton singlet or tank top with a deep scoop neck, ribbed knit, and a small woven label on the hem, in white, navy, grey, pale blue and cream. The famous baby wondersuit is a one-piece zip-front romper with small cuffs and stretch cotton jersey, printed with simple cheerful motifs, stripes, polka dots, small animals, clouds, stars and kangaroo-like characters in soft pastel yellow, mint, sky blue and pink. The logo is a simple lowercase or capital block sans-serif in a flat colour, sometimes a small blue box with white letters. Packaging is clean and bright, with plastic-wrapped packs in white and bright blue and a bold clear design. The mood is clean, soft, honest and dependable, down-to-earth with a friendly sense of humour.
In the style of Crown Lynn, New Zealand mid-century ceramics. Everyday tableware is thick, durable and rounded, including heavy white cups, bowls, plates, jugs and teapots in a glossy glaze, along with the famous swan vases with an elegant curved neck and a hollow back, produced in white, matte pastel, pink, turquoise and black. Glazes include speckled turquoise, mustard yellow, tangerine, sage green, a dripping trickle glaze where one colour runs over another, and dark manganese brown with scratched-line sgraffito patterns. Decals show modern abstract florals, bamboo, diamond shapes, starburst, ferns and Maori-inspired koru spirals in mid-century colours. A classic cafe set has a deep brown-and-turquoise cup with a chunky handle and shallow saucer. Shapes are plump, rounded and friendly with simple lines. The mood is retro, warm, optimistic and distinctly 1950s and 1960s New Zealand.
In the style of Fisher and Paykel, New Zealand domestic appliances. Washing machines and fridges are clean, boxy, rounded-corner white enamel cabinets, from the 1950s through the 1970s with a thin chrome strip, a simple round dial and a stylised metal badge. Later designs are bright white or stainless steel with a slim control panel, a smooth top, large soft-touch rounded dials, a gentle blue or green backlit display and neat vertical or horizontal handles of brushed steel. The signature is the double-drawer dishwasher, two slim stainless steel drawers stacked in a cabinet, with a recessed handle and a narrow screen. Ranges are plain stainless steel with big oven doors in black glass, chunky round knobs in silver and a plain, modern seamless profile. The mood is practical, quiet, friendly and design-led, in white, silver and grey with minimal ornament.
In the style of Weta Workshop, a New Zealand creature and prop studio. Pieces look convincingly real and handmade, such as battle-worn medieval and fantasy armour, helmets with scratched edges, sculpted swords, shields, rune-etched blades, leather bracers, mail shirts with heavy ring links and weathered cloaks. Surfaces show distressed paint, rust-brown stains, dull pewter, tarnished bronze and bone-white enamel, with scratches, dents, mud streaks and worn leather straps. Creatures are massive and sculpted with pores, veins, wrinkles, hairs, tusks and wet glossy eyes, in silicone skin painted with subtle layers of olive, brown, ochre and grey. A sibling line of retro ray guns and steampunk blasters are fat chunky brass and copper rocket-like objects with dials, fins, vacuum tubes and lettering. The look is gritty, textured, painterly, detailed and storybook-epic.
In the style of Zhostovo painted metal trays from the Moscow region of Russia. Trays are thin sheet-metal, usually oval, rectangular or heart-shaped with a gently curving raised rim. The ground is glossy black lacquer, though some are red, blue, green or white. A lush bouquet of large garden and wild flowers sits in the middle, such as roses, peonies, tulips, dahlias, poppies, cornflowers and daisies, loosely tossed with leaves, buds and fruit and painted in a few quick full brush strokes with a soft, shaded modelling, in oil colours of deep crimson, pink, orange, yellow, blue and fresh green against the dark ground. A smaller scatter of blossoms and berries is arranged around the main bunch. The rim is edged with gold or silver decorative scrolls, dots and little leaf ornaments. The final surface is polished to a mirror shine under several layers of lacquer. The mood is lush, sumptuous, festive and warm.
In the style of Pavlovo Posad printed wool shawls from Russia. Each shawl is a large soft square of fine wool or wool-silk, folded diagonally and worn on the head or shoulders, bordered by long knotted silk fringe and edged by a wide band of repeating pattern. The field is a deep black, scarlet red, emerald green, navy, ivory or cream background covered with an exuberant arrangement of big full-blown cabbage roses, peonies, tulips and leaves, with smaller sprigs and buds in delicate patterns. The roses are shaded in raspberry, pink, orange and white with green and turquoise leaves, outlined with fine lines and with a hand-printed, slightly soft-edged look. The borders show paisley and oriental-style curls, garlands and scrollwork. Some shawls are a plain solid colour with a rose border only. The mood is folk-luxe, rich, floral, romantic and decorative, redolent of old Russian peasant festivals.
In the style of Dymkovo clay toys from the Vyatka region of Russia. Figures are hand-moulded from red clay, fired, then coated in a bright milky-white chalk base that gives a smooth matte porcelain-like finish. They show plump stylised women in long bell-shaped skirts, ladies with parasols, nursemaids, water-carriers, horse riders, cockerels, rams, turkeys, deer and sleigh groups, with simple rounded bodies, tiny heads and exaggerated puffy collars and sleeves. Painting is crisp and playful in bright poster-like colours, red, orange, yellow, blue, green and black, in large circles, checks, diagonal stripes, wavy lines, dots and rhombuses on the dresses, with rosy circular cheeks and small black dot eyes. Details such as hair, hats, tails and horns are picked out in glittering gold leaf. The look is cheerful, flat, geometric and slightly naive, like a patterned doll made of ceramic icing.
In the style of Fedoskino lacquer miniatures from Russia. The objects are papier-mache boxes, caskets, brooches, egg shapes and plaques, coated in layer upon layer of glossy black lacquer, and the inside is often bright red or vermilion. The lid carries a small finely detailed oil painting in realistic style, depicting troika sleighs rushing through snow, a family drinking tea at a samovar, a village winter scene, a fairy-tale princess, a river landscape, a feast, or a bouquet of flowers. The painting uses glazing in many translucent layers so figures glow with soft light, and the backgrounds often have a shimmering gold, silver leaf or mother-of-pearl underlayer which makes the sky glow warmly. Edges may be framed with gold ornament, braids and flowers. The overall finish is mirror-smooth, deep and jewel-like in black, crimson, gold and sky blue. The mood is polished, storytelling and luxurious.
In the style of the Soviet Ukrainian ZAZ Zaporozhets microcar. The car is tiny, boxy and rounded, a two-door rear-engined saloon about three metres long with a short stubby bonnet, a steep sloping or humped rear deck with large cooling air intakes on the rear flanks, small round headlights set in simple chrome-ringed bezels, a plain narrow grille or a handful of vertical slats, thin chrome bumpers and a tiny roofline. Rear-hinged doors, slim pillars and a big curved windscreen give it an airy look. Paint is flat cheap-looking gloss in pea green, cream, sky blue, light yellow, orange-red, brick red and grey, often with a thin chrome side strip. Wheels are small, with plain hubcaps and narrow tyres. The interior is spartan with a single round speedometer and thin metal dash. The mood is humble, endearing, utilitarian and drably cheerful.
In the style of Petrykivka folk painting from central Ukraine. The decoration is a lavish flat floral ornament painted freehand onto white walls, wooden chests, plates, trays, eggs, boxes and furniture. Flowers are stylised and fantastical, such as large asters, dahlias, poppies, roses and tulips, shown flat from above or side-on, with fern-like leaves, curling tendrils, berries such as viburnum, tiny grape clusters and the occasional firebird, rooster or peacock. Brush strokes are visible and clearly shaped, a comb stroke, a seed, a nut, a teardrop, a tapering curl, and berries are made with a fingertip dab, giving a lighter centre ringed with a darker edge. Colours are strong and clear on a white, black, red, yellow or deep blue ground, with scarlet, cobalt, magenta, orange, lemon yellow and leaf green. Compositions are symmetrical and gracefully balanced, with a lively and celebratory feel.
In the style of Antonov, Ukrainian aircraft design. Aircraft are rugged, utilitarian and enormous, with a high wing set on a deep boxy fuselage, a glazed navigator nose with a downward-looking bulge, an upswept rear cargo ramp, and multiple small wheel bogies in a long row under bulged fairings on the belly. The large transports have double or twin tail fins, four wide engines under the wings, and a giant slab body painted in simple colours, matte military grey or white with a blue and yellow cheatline, a thin red or blue stripe and plain block lettering in Cyrillic and Latin script. A small classic biplane has fabric-covered wings with struts and wires, and a big radial engine in a rounded nose cowl, painted silver, green or orange. The look is heavy, honest, workmanlike and spectacular in scale.
In the style of Boleslawiec stoneware from Poland. The body is a thick, dense, glossy-glazed cream or white stoneware with a heavy hand-thrown feel. Pieces include mugs with big strap handles, teapots, pitchers, bowls, plates, jars, butter dishes and egg cups. The decoration is stamped by hand with cut sponges dipped in cobalt blue underglaze, producing clusters of tiny dots, peacock-eye circles, small florals, feathery sprigs, scallops, windmill patterns and rings of leaves in rhythmic repeats. The main colour is deep cobalt blue on a white ground, with a secondary palette of green, yellow, brown, rust and purple used for flowers and borders. The glaze is clear, smooth and shiny with a slightly uneven pooling at the foot, and the rim often has a bold stripe of blue. The look is rustic, cheerful, tidy and cottage-like, familiar from every country kitchen shelf.
In the style of the Polish School of Posters, post-war hand-painted film and theatre posters. The image is an expressive, surreal and witty painted or collaged illustration, typically a single bold metaphor instead of a literal scene, such as a giant eye, a flower with a thorn, a distorted face, a bird with a human body, a single broken object, or a childlike doodle on an empty space. Colours are saturated and unusual, such as acid yellow, blood red, deep blue, hot pink, mustard, turquoise and black, with ragged brushwork, gouache texture, torn paper edges and uneven print texture. Lettering is hand-drawn, quirky, irregular and integrated into the composition, in large loose letters in a contrasting colour. The format is a tall portrait sheet with roughly textured paper. The mood is poetic, dark, humorous, expressive and arty.
In the style of the Polish FSO Syrena. This is a small, upright, rounded two-door saloon from the 1950s and 1960s with a bulbous bonnet, a smooth two-box body, a notched or fastback tail and separate bulging front wings. The first version has a high slim roof, small round headlamps flush with the body, a short plain chrome grille with a few horizontal bars, thin chrome bumpers, small door handles and rear-hinged suicide doors. Later versions have a restyled front with a squarer grille, bigger lights, a lowered waistline and a larger rear window. Paint is plain glossy pastel, such as cream, sky blue, pistachio green, pale grey, light red and mustard, with chrome trim along the flanks and a small crest on the nose. The interior has a bench seat, a simple metal dash, a big thin steering wheel and a small speedometer. The mood is modest, cute and period-correct socialist-era motoring.
In the style of Jawa, Czechoslovak motorcycles. The bike has a compact two-stroke engine with finned cylinder and a rounded engine casing in silver, a teardrop fuel tank with a chrome knee pad side panel and a bold enamel badge, a flat bench seat, a swept-back handlebar, a round chrome headlamp, and thin tubular steel frame. Paint is two-tone glossy enamel, such as maroon and cream, black and red, deep blue and white, or olive and cream with fine hand-painted gold pinstripes on the tank. Twin chrome exhaust pipes flare slightly at the end like tapered trumpets. Mudguards are deep and curved, wheels are wire-spoked with chrome rims and a small chromed hub brake. Electrics are in a round chrome-ringed headlight with a bulb and a small instrument pod. The look is solid, simple, mid-century and slightly sporty, with plenty of chrome and shine.
In the style of Koh-i-noor Hardtmuth, Czech pencils and drawing materials. The signature pencil is a hexagonal cedar-wood barrel lacquered in a strong yellow, with a gold or silver stamped logo on the face and the grade printed in small neat capitals in a band of hot-stamped foil. The top is dipped in dark green or black. Packaging includes slim flat cardboard boxes or tins in yellow, with a diamond icon and a crest on the lid, with ornate serif lettering and fine linework. The drawing sets contain coloured pencils in tin cases with a hinged lid and rows of 12 to 36 colours, a sharpened point and crisp lacquered barrels each in a matching hue with a gold ring. Erasers are rectangular blocks, rubbery cream and pink. The look is orderly, precise, industrial-vintage and nicely engineered.
In the style of Herend porcelain from Hungary. The body is fine hard-paste porcelain, bright white and translucent, with a high gloss. Tableware, vases, tureens and animal figures are hand-painted and gilded in very fine detail. The best-known decoration is a Chinese-inspired bouquet of flowering branches with fluttering butterflies in soft pastel tints, with a lattice-work border, a pierced openwork basket-weave on edges, and a thin gold rim. Other patterns include a green-and-gold scale, a pink-and-red bamboo, a rose-and-forget-me-not garland, and deep cobalt blue panels. Animal figures such as foxes, rabbits, cats, birds and fish are covered in a dense net of tiny crackle-like fishscale or fish-net pattern in rust red, turquoise, lilac, green, or gold. Handles are twisted into branches and knobs are modelled like fruits or flowers. The look is refined, elaborate, delicate and old-world, like a rich aristocratic dinner service.
In the style of Zsolnay of Pecs in Hungary, Art Nouveau iridescent ceramics. The signature is an eosin glaze, a shimmering metallic lustre which shifts between gold, green, magenta, purple, cobalt and copper-red as the light changes, like the surface of a beetle wing or an oil slick. Vases, bowls, jugs and figures have sinuous sculpted forms, swelling bellies, flared necks, whiplash curves and applied handles shaped like tendrils and lizards. Decoration is relief-modelled flowers, leaves, dragonflies, peacock feathers, seaweed, faces and Oriental motifs, with matte backgrounds and glossy iridescence on selected areas. Architectural pieces are frost-proof pyrogranite roof tiles and facade ornaments, in glazed warm colours of orange, green, turquoise, ochre and cobalt blue, often arranged in colourful tile roof patterns of scales and chevrons. The overall mood is lavish, jewel-like, organic and fin-de-siecle.
In the style of Matyo folk embroidery of Mezokovesd in Hungary. Embroidery is worked in dense, thick, glossy cotton or silk thread on black, white or dark blue linen and cotton cloth, on blouses, aprons, tablecloths, cushion covers, pillowcases and scarves. The main motifs are huge, full-blown roses, bold tulips, carnations, leaf sprays, buds, and tendrils in a heavily stylised form, often with a central flower surrounded by several smaller blossoms and swirling leaves, in mirrored symmetry. The palette is bright, high-contrast and joyful, with red, pink, magenta, orange, yellow, light blue and green on black, or navy, crimson and deep green on white. Stitches are tight satin stitch with a slightly raised, padded texture and often black or gold outlines. The look is richly decorated, colourful, joyous and festive, as if a flower garden was embroidered over every surface.
In the style of Halas lace from Hungary. This is a fine, hand-made needle lace stitched with a single needle and fine thread on a fixed pattern outline. Pieces are collars, doilies, table mats, bookmarks, cuffs, fans and framed panels, with a dense, even, light-as-air lace structure, tiny buttonhole stitches that create a solid motif surrounded by open meshes, and picots on the edges. Motifs are stylised Hungarian folk flowers such as tulips, roses and carnations, bell flowers, heart shapes, leaves, buds and vines, along with birds, symmetrical lozenges and scalloped borders. The colour is traditionally off-white or cream, but historical pieces appear in soft peach, pale orange, light green and butter yellow silk. The look is fine, airy, geometric-floral, delicate and graceful, with a handmade crispness, resembling frost patterns.
In the style of Zastava, the Yugoslav car maker of the 1980s. Cars are small, square-cut, boxy hatchbacks and saloons with a flat, near-vertical nose, a thin black plastic grille, rectangular headlamps, black plastic bumpers and trim, a short bonnet, a pronounced shoulder line, flat side windows, and a hatchback with a sharply cut tail. Door handles are plain black, wheels have small steel rims with simple plastic or steel caps. Paint is plain gloss colour, such as red, sky blue, white, yellow, silver-grey, beige or orange, sometimes with a thin bright stripe. A small badge with a stylised Z sits on the grille. Interiors are plain, with hard plastic dashboards in brown or black, cloth seats in plaid or stripes. The mood is cheap, honest, utilitarian and distinctively late socialist era.
In the style of Penkala, Croatian fountain pens and mechanical pencils. The pens are slim cylindrical writing instruments with a hard rubber or celluloid barrel in glossy black, mottled red-and-black, pale marbled tones, dark blue or olive, with gold-plated or nickel bands near the cap, a tapered cap end, a long clip with a small ball tip, and a gold nib with a tiny scroll engraving. The mechanical pencils are metal tubes with fine ribbed or knurled grips, silver or gold finish, geometric engraved stripes, and a long sliding mechanism, often with an elegant tassel loop. Boxes are small hinged cases lined with velvet in dark green or burgundy, with a gold-stamped lid. The look is precise, early Art Deco, slim and quietly luxurious. Everything is made with a hand-finished glow, smooth seams, tiny engraved lines and a small stamped maker mark near the clip, giving a jewel-like desk object feel in muted period colours.
In the style of Olympic Airways, the Greek airline of the 1960s and 1970s. The aircraft has a gleaming white upper fuselage and polished silver belly divided by a navy blue cheatline with a thin sky blue stripe running along the windows, large dark blue capital letters in a smart modern sans-serif and a Greek script version beside them. The tail is plain white or deep blue with five interlocking rings in a ring emblem in crisp contrasting colours, sometimes stacked as a vertical sign. Cabin staff wore sleek mod uniforms by a Paris couturier, with short skirts in navy, white and sky blue and tall boots. The timetables and posters show sun-washed Aegean scenes, white temples, blue domes, fishing boats, columns, sparkling sea and bright blue sky. The mood is glamorous, sunny and jet-set.
In the style of the Haapsalu lace shawl from Estonia. A rectangular or triangular scarf is knitted in extremely fine, cobweb-thin lamb wool or wool-silk yarn, so light that it can be drawn through a ring. The shawl is made of a central field, a border and a separate edge sewn on. Patterns are airy open-work lace motifs, nupp bobbles, diamond lozenges, leaf shapes, fir cones, lily-of-the-valley bells, snowflakes, ripples, and a scalloped, saw-toothed edge with a dense row of tiny points. Colour is traditionally natural white or ivory, sometimes pale grey, soft peach or faded rose. The texture is fuzzy, misty and ethereal, with a delicate halo of loose fibres, and hung against a dark background it resembles frost on a window or cobwebs in morning dew. The look is delicate, geometric and romantic, handmade with mathematical patience.
In the style of Avanos red clay pottery in Cappadocia in Turkey. The clay is dug from the red silt of the river, giving a rich terracotta, brick-red and dark brown body. Pieces are thrown on a foot-powered kick wheel, often by hand, such as wide-bellied amphora, narrow-necked water jugs, pitchers, plates, lidded casseroles, wine jugs and large ornamental platters. Decoration is applied with slip, incised lines and glaze, with spirals, hatched bands, Hittite sun discs, tulip and carnation flowers, stylised birds, geometric borders, zigzags and dots in cream, brown, turquoise, green and cobalt blue. The surface can be unglazed matte, or covered with a glossy lead-free glaze in green or amber, showing cracks and pooled colour. Some jugs are shaped like a face or a bird. The look is warm, earthy, ancient and handmade.
In the style of Roots, a Canadian outdoor lifestyle brand. The logo is a simple beaver standing on a small group of branches, with the name underneath in a slightly rounded vintage serif typeface, often embroidered in a bright colour on grey marl sweatshirts. Sweatshirts and sweatpants are thick, soft heather-grey fleece with bold chest lettering, cuffs and kangaroo pockets. Leather goods include the Tribe bag, a soft saddle-brown leather tote and bucket bag with a stamped logo and a thick strap, along with leather varsity jackets with wool bodies and leather sleeves, and the famous Negative Heel shoe with a flat, thick, roomy base. Colours are earthy and rustic, with forest green, burgundy, navy, camel, cream, plaid red and black and maple-red accents. The mood is warm, casual, woodsy and cabin-in-the-woods.
In the style of Canada Goose, extreme-cold parkas. The parka is a heavy, thigh-length down-filled coat with a large padded hood trimmed with a fur ruff, a tall double collar, a two-way front zip covered by a big storm flap closed with snaps, big flapped patch pockets at hip level, elasticated storm cuffs and an adjustable cinch waist. The fabric is dense matte cotton-nylon with a slightly rough weave, in black, navy, graphite grey, sand, red or arctic white, and quilted baffles are sometimes visible. The arm has a round badge sewn on the sleeve, a roundel with a polar map and the name in red text around it. Details include reflective piping, a leather zipper pull and a bold tag on the chest. The silhouette is puffy, boxy, wide-shouldered and protective, with a rugged utilitarian polar expedition look.
In the style of Ski-Doo snowmobiles of the 1960s and 1970s. The machine is a compact, low sled with a bulbous moulded fibreglass hood that swoops down towards two short front skis, a high bubble windscreen, an upswept ducktail seat, a pair of caterpillar tracks and exposed suspension springs. The paint is bright and glossy, a sunny yellow, orange, red, white or lime green, with bold contrasting stripes, large block letters and a decal of a stylised animal or a speed lines graphic. Chrome parts include a small bumper, a round headlight and shiny handlebar grips. The track is black rubber with ribs, the skis are chrome-edged metal with a slight curl, and the rear has a chunky tail light. Riders wear one-piece quilted snowsuits in matching bright colours. In the style of Rorstrand, Swedish mid-century porcelain. The best-known pattern is a white porcelain tableware with softly shaded cobalt-blue flowers painted on the rim and inside, with loose organic leaf outlines and a soft airbrush-like gradient, accompanied by a plain curving silhouette. Cups have a slender round handle, saucers are shallow, plates have a thin slightly rounded rim, and tureens have domed lids with simple knob handles. Other patterns offer a rustic mottled blue-and-white, green-and-brown speckled stoneware, and a trailing line of blue stripes. Earlier designs show Purple Lace transfer prints, a chinoiserie willow scene and a spray of green leaves. The overall look is gentle, light, sophisticated, calm and domestic, with the quiet modernism of Scandinavian dining, in a restrained palette of white, blue, soft green and brown.
In the style of Gustavsberg, Swedish mid-century studio ceramics. Pieces are hand-thrown or moulded vases, bowls, dishes, ashtrays, tiles and stoneware figurines in soft, curved and slightly lopsided organic shapes, such as pebble forms, leaf dishes, squashed spheres and wide-hipped jugs. Glazes are matte and richly mottled, in sage green, rust, mustard, deep turquoise, charcoal grey and cream, often with sgraffito, incised stripes and dots, circles, linear figures, animal motifs and silhouetted leaves. A signature type is a dark green-brown stoneware inlaid with silver, formed in rounded jars with a delicate silver leaf pattern. Tableware is plain white with coloured berries and flowers. The mood is warm, humane, playful and Scandinavian modern, with intimate handmade surfaces. Surfaces show glaze pooling at the edges, visible finger marks, soft shoulders on every vase and a calm, slightly earthy palette that suits teak shelves and linen curtains.
In the style of Svenskt Tenn, Swedish interior house. The fabrics are printed in large-scale botanical and fantastical patterns on white or cream linen and cotton, with detailed lush flowers, leaves, vines, birds, butterflies, maps and exotic fruit in saturated, slightly unexpected colours such as teal, mustard, coral, raspberry, tangerine and deep green on a pale background, drawn with hand-painted freedom. Furniture is light, comfortable and curvy, with spindly legs, rounded corners and woven cane seats, in mahogany or pale birch, with glass-fronted cabinets, brass details, painted chests and wall-hung shelves. Pewter, glass and brass objects are plain and sculptural. Rooms are eclectic and sunny, mixing patterns, plants, and warm wood with an intentionally unplanned look. The mood is cheerful, bright, cultured, and deliberately casual.
In the style of the Dala horse of Dalarna, Sweden. The figure is a small stout wooden horse carved from pine in simple flat planes, with a thick arched neck, a short mane cut into notches, a straight stiff tail, four sturdy legs on a flat base and a bulky chest. The body is painted a glossy deep red or orange-red, though some are blue, black, white or natural. The harness, saddle and bridle are decorated by hand with freehand flower scroll patterns in white, green, yellow and blue, with brush strokes in a flower and swirl vocabulary known as kurbits, painted quickly in loops, petals and dots along the neck and flanks. Sizes range from a palm-size souvenir to a giant roadside version. The finish is smooth, shiny and lightly worn, and the look is simple, friendly, folk-rustic and unmistakably Scandinavian.
In the style of Dale of Norway, Scandinavian wool sweaters. The garment is a thick, close-knit wool pullover or cardigan with a high collar or round neck, and a patterned yoke pattern worked in stranded colourwork, a circular band of stars, snowflakes, diamonds, reindeer, zigzags and stepped borders around the chest and shoulders. Colours are strong, with navy, red, white and grey as the classic combination, plus charcoal, forest green and cream. Ribbed cuffs, hems and neckbands are plain, and cardigans have pewter clasps or metal buttons. The wool is dense and slightly fuzzy, and the pattern is clear and graphic with neat repeating bands. The sports sweaters carry the national flag colours and ring motifs. The mood is crisp, alpine, snowy and cosy. The garment has a snug, athletic fit and shows crisp zigzag edging, tiny star motifs in white and a thick shawl or turtle collar, as seen on alpine ski slopes in the 1960s and 1980s.
In the style of Hadeland Glassverk, Norwegian hand-blown glass. The glassware is clear, thick and slightly bubbled, mouth-blown into rounded organic shapes such as heavy tumblers, wide bowls, tall stemmed vases, candle lights and pitchers, with lightly wobbly lines. Colours are cool and soft, such as pale smoky grey, aqua green, ice-blue, amber, deep cobalt and deep purple, with swirled layers, trapped air bubbles and uneven rims. Some pieces are cut with simple vertical grooves or concentric rings, others engraved with fir branches or snowflakes. Mid-century pieces have fluid free-form silhouettes, such as leaf-shaped bowls and asymmetrical vases. The mood is clean, cold, bright and Nordic, evoking frozen lakes and fjords. Lighting through the glass creates soft pools of colour on tabletops, and the overall impression is hushed, handmade, a little irregular and unmistakably Scandinavian.
In the style of 66 North, Icelandic weatherproof outerwear. Jackets and parkas are bold, practical, hooded shells with a high collar, a storm flap over the zipper, taped seams, large flapped pockets, adjustable cuffs and drawcord hems, made of matte waterproof fabric in strong colours, such as bright red, orange, deep blue, black, moss green and ochre against dark mountain backdrops. Down jackets are heavily quilted into long baffles, wool sweaters use stranded yoke patterns in grey, cream and charcoal, and the small logo is a tiny fixed-pointed badge or tag with the name in plain capital letters. The cut is long, broad and roomy, like arctic fishermen clothing refined into minimalism. The mood is austere, volcanic, windy and functional. Fabrics show a subtle sheen under rain, with chunky zip pulls, bold hood peaks and a plain functional layering approach, photographed against black sand, moss and low cloud.
In the style of Fiskars, Finnish scissors and garden tools. The signature object is a pair of scissors with bright orange moulded plastic handles in a smooth, open, rounded loop shape and silver stainless steel blades with a satin finish, joined by a flat black or orange pivot screw. Handles are ergonomic, with a soft thumb curve and a slightly textured or ridged inner surface, and the blades carry fine laser-etched lettering. Garden tools such as axes, loppers, saws and pruners have orange fibreglass or plastic handles with black grips, a clean taper and a sleek shape, with a razor-bright metal head. Packaging is orange and black with a clean sans-serif logotype. The mood is functional, friendly, clean and instantly recognisable, with a single hot orange accent.
In the style of Vista Alegre, Portuguese porcelain. The body is thin, bright white hard-paste porcelain with a smooth shiny glaze. Tableware has decorations drawn from aristocratic traditions, with gold rims, fine landscape vignettes in sepia or blue, delicate floral sprays, garlands of roses, medallions, heraldic crests, Greek-key borders and raised relief, while service pieces include soup tureens, teapots, platters and cake stands with scrolling handles, high-footed shapes and finials in the form of flowers. Modern collections turn to bold graphic prints, hand-painted strokes in cobalt, black or coral, and sculptural art editions. The traditional palette is white, gold, navy, rose pink and sage green. The look is elegant, formal, light and slightly romantic. Backstamps and tiny gilt crowns appear on the bases, and sets are laid out in generous, symmetrical table settings with white linen, silver cutlery and crystal glass.
In the style of the Caldas da Rainha ceramic factory in Portugal, naturalistic majolica. Plates, bowls, tureens, jugs and platters are moulded in the form of oversized cabbage leaves, with veined, ruffled edges and glossy, vivid green glaze, with a pale ribbed back. Other pieces are modelled as realistic lobsters, crabs, snakes, frogs, lizards, fish, snails, beetles, flies and sunflowers, crawling across plates, ewers and jugs in lifelike detail. Glazes are glossy, juicy and saturated, with leaf green, mustard yellow, turquoise, coral, tomato red, aubergine purple and cream. The colours are exaggerated, the textures are lumpy and tactile, and the humour is cheeky. The look is whimsical, sculptural, rustic and slightly surreal, designed for a very Portuguese table. Colours glisten as though freshly wet, and pieces cluster on rustic tables among real vegetables, shellfish and wicker, with a humorous, almost trompe loeil effect.
In the style of Viuva Lamego, Portuguese azulejo tile house. The tiles are square glazed ceramic tiles about fifteen centimetres wide, with glossy surface and slightly uneven edges. Walls and facades are covered in complete panels, mostly in cobalt blue on a creamy white ground, depicting baroque scenes with scrolling acanthus, cherubs, saints, garden scenes, ships, harbours, hunts, rural festivals and tiled frames made of swags and volutes. Other tiles show repeating patterns of geometric lozenges, checkers, flowers and diamonds in blue, yellow, green and manganese purple. Art Nouveau tiles have curving plants, lilies, irises, stylised maidens and delicate gold-green outlines. Railway stations, church walls, shop fronts and fountains are clad in these panels. The look is cool, bright, narrative and decorative, with a serene seaside light.
In the style of Claus Porto, Portuguese soap and perfumery packaging. Each bar of soap is wrapped in thick paper with a lavishly patterned lithographic print in the Art Deco and Belle Epoque style, such as peacock feathers, ferns, roses, swallows, geometric fans, arcs and rays. Colours are rich and layered, with teal and gold, burgundy and cream, coral and navy, emerald and gold, with metallic foil stamping, a fine coloured border and a central oval or square label with ornate lettering in elegant serif and decorative capitals, and a tiny crest or ribbon. The soaps are heavy, smooth, pale creamy rectangles, embossed with a crest. Bottles are rounded glass in a vintage pharmacy style with a stopper. The shop interior has a high ceiling, wooden shelves, and colourful rows of soap boxes. The mood is nostalgic, glamorous, and carefully crafted.
In the style of Lladro, Spanish porcelain figurines. The figures are tall, graceful and elongated, representing young women with parasols, shepherd girls, dancers, ballerinas, children with geese, nuns, clowns, horses and swans, with slim bodies, long limbs, small heads and flowing, swirling robes. Faces are serene, with softly closed or gentle eyes, a pale rosy complexion and tiny delicate hands. The porcelain is mostly matte, chalky and soft, in pastel colours such as pale blue, grey-green, lilac, rose, cream, white and muted gold, with occasional glossy accents and fine hand-applied porcelain flowers. Forms are fluid and somewhat stretched, with the folds of clothes made into rippling, wavy ribbons. A glazed gres line has deeper earthy tones. The look is sentimental, romantic, delicate and fragile. The bases are small and rounded, with tiny flowers and ribbons, the poses are quiet and tender, and displays use soft spotlighting in glass cabinets with mirrored backs.
In the style of Camper, Mallorcan footwear. The shoes are quirky, comfortable, rounded and chunky, such as stitched leather sneakers with rounded toes, thick soles and vulcanised edges, and a rubber sole with a graphic design of bumps, circles or a wavy tread. Pelotas have a sole with tiny nubbles like a tennis ball. Twins are mismatched pairs in different colours or details, each foot visibly different. Uppers are soft leather or canvas in muted tones of brown, beige, white, grey, navy and olive, with a splash of red, yellow or green, and visible contrast stitching. Details include a simple small tag with the name, white laces and an opening of rounded padding. Boxes are plain cardboard in white and brown. The mood is playful, down-to-earth, relaxed and a bit nerdy.
In the style of Desigual, Barcelona fashion brand. The clothes are an exuberant, loud, joyful collage of prints, such as patchwork panels stitched from different floral, geometric and animal fabrics, bright embroidery, appliques, studs, sequins, lace inserts and ragged edges. Dresses and jackets mix several patterns on the same garment, such as colourful hearts, butterflies, flowers, zebra stripes, ethnic motifs, mosaic tiles, polka dots, graffiti and cartoon-like faces, in a riot of colours, turquoise, fuchsia, orange, lime, red, royal blue and yellow against black or white. Denim jackets have a worn-in painted look, bags are covered in stitched sections, and tags carry a big stylised swirling logo. The mood is optimistic, artistic, free, bohemian and colourful. Garment shapes stay simple, such as shirt dresses, bomber jackets and wrap tops, so the busy print mix carries the whole look, with mismatched buttons and playful contrast linings.
In the style of Chupa Chups, Spanish lollipops. The sweet is a round ball candy on a white paper stick, wrapped in thin shiny plastic twisted at the base, with a label in the centre on top. The logo is a bright flower, a daisy-like shape with a yellow circle in the middle and red petals, with the name in friendly rounded white and yellow letters across it, on a bright red, yellow or orange background. Flavours are marked by colours, such as pink strawberry, orange, green apple, cola brown and lemon yellow, and some sweets are swirled with two colours. Display stands are hot-pink or red and shaped like a round tree, with dozens of lollipops pointing outwards in a sunburst. Posters show cartoon children licking lollipops. The mood is playful, sugary, cheeky and very pop.
In the style of Mil, Soviet helicopters. Machines are large, chunky and utilitarian, with a bulbous pod-like cabin, a round glazed nose, big round windows, a long tapering tail boom with a small stabiliser, a massive five-bladed rotor on a thick hub, and two big engines on top, exposed in a boxy cowl with exhaust pipes. Wheels are fixed on stubby struts. The paint is plain, either military olive-drab and matte green with stencil markings, or civil white and sky blue with a red star or a winged emblem and a broad blue or red stripe, with large black Cyrillic lettering and a registration code. Panels show riveted seams, patches, rows of screws and inspection hatches, and the surface looks hardworking and slightly weathered. The mood is rugged, heavy and Cold War.
In the style of Petrof, Czech pianos. Grand and upright pianos have deep, glossy black polished cases, with smooth curved sides, a plain tall lid, rounded cheeks and thick tapered legs on brass castors. The lid is opened on a stick to show an arrangement of cast-iron plate in gold bronze, strings running at an angle and felt hammers. The name is shown in gold letters in the fallboard or on a small brass plate on the front. Alternative finishes include warm walnut, mahogany or white lacquer, with a matte or satin sheen. Upright pianos have a plain front with a music desk and simple candle brackets in older models. The keys are ivory-white with ebony black sharps, and the pedals are brass. The look is solid, dignified, classical and carefully made.
